is equivalent to the trivial loop via backtracking relations, it holds that
f(P_{m})=Id
. We get that
f(P_{0})=Id
, that is,
f(P_{0})=f(P_{w})^{-1}\circ f(u,w)\circ f(P_{u})=Id
. Moving this around we get that

f(u,w)=g_{C}(w)g_{C}(u)^{-1}={\delta}g_{C}(u,w).

∎

In light of
Claim
4.1.1
, and looking ahead to a case where most triangles
uvw
satisfy
{\delta}f(uvw)=Id
but not all, it seems as though the fewer triangles we have in the contraction, the better. In light of this we define

diam(C)=\max_{uw\in\overset{\to}{X}(1)}\lvert T_{uw}\rvert.

4.2
Coboundary Expansion from Cones

We are now ready to prove
Lemma
1.6
. In fact, we prove this theorem in more generality. Let
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a family of cones. The cone distribution
D_{\mathcal{C}}
is the following distribution over
\{u,v,w\}\in X(2)
:

1.

Sample an edge
uw\in\overset{\to}{X}(1)
.

2.

Sample a cone
C_{i}
uniformly at random.

3.

Sample some
P_{j}\in T_{uw}
.

4.

Output the triangle
t_{P_{j},P_{j+1}}
.

We denote by
D_{\mathcal{C}}(uvw)
the probability of sampling
uvw
according to this distribution.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
p\in(0,1)
and let
R\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
X
be a simplicial complex, and denote by
\mu_{X}
the distribution over triangles of
X
. Suppose
X
has a family of cones
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that:

1.

(D_{\mathcal{C}},\mu_{X})
are
p
-smooth.

2.

\max_{i\in I}diam(C_{i})\leqslant R
.

Then
h^{1}_{r}(X)\geqslant\frac{p}{R}
.

For example, suppose
C
is a cone, and
Aut(X)
acts transitively on
k
-faces of
X
. This means that for any fixed triangle
T_{0}\in X(2)
and
k
-face containing it
F_{0}\supset T_{0}
, when we choose a uniformly random element
\phi\in Aut(X)
then
\phi(F_{0})
is a uniformly random
k
-face.
\phi(T_{0})
is not necessarily distributed uniformly in
X(2)
, but it captures at least
1/\binom{k+1}{3}
fraction of the probability space of triangles.
Thus if
\mathcal{C}=\{\sigma.C\}_{\sigma\in Aut(X)}
, then
D_{\mathcal{C}}
is
p=\frac{1}{\binom{k+1}{3}}
-smooth, and so
Lemma
4.3
immediately implies
Lemma
1.6
.

Proof of
Lemma
4.3
.

Fix
f\in C^{1}
. We need to find some
g\in C^{0}
such that
\dist(f,{\delta}g)\leqslant Rp^{-1}wt({\delta}f)
.

It is enough to show that
\E_{{i\in I}}\left[\dist(f,{\delta}g_{C_{i}})\right]\leqslant Rp^{-1}wt(f)
, since this in particular proves that there is some
g_{C_{i}}
that acheives the expectation.
Indeed

\E_{{i\in I}}\left[\dist(f,{\delta}g_{C_{i}})\right]=\E_{{i\in I}}\left[\ProbOp_{{uv\in\overset{\to}{X}(1)}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g_{C_{i}}(uv)\right]\right]

and by
Claim
4.1.1
this is upper bounded by

\E_{{i\in I}}\left[\ProbOp_{{uv\in\overset{\to}{X}(1)}}\left[\exists P_{\ell}\in T_{uw}\;{\delta}f(t_{P_{\ell},P_{\ell+1}})\neq Id\right]\right].

There are at most
R
paths in every cone, hence if there exists such a pair, the probability that we uniformly sample such a pair is at least
\frac{1}{R}
. Hence this expression is upper bounded by

R\cdot\E_{{i\in I}}\left[\ProbOp_{{uv\in\overset{\to}{X}(1),P_{\ell}\in T_{uw}}}\left[{\delta}f(t_{P_{\ell},P_{\ell+1}})\neq Id\right]\right]=R\cdot\ProbOp_{{t\sim D_{\mathcal{C}}}}\left[{\delta}f(t)\neq Id\right].

(4.1)

The probability of every event according to the distribution
D_{\mathcal{C}}
is at most
p^{-1}
its probability according to the distribution of the triangles of
X
. Hence, (
4.1
) is at most

Rp^{-1}\cdot\ProbOp_{{uvw\in\overset{\to}{X}(2)}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq Id\right]=Rp^{-1}wt({\delta}f).

The lemma follows.
∎

5
The GK decomposition

We describe here a very interesting technique due to recent work of Gotlib and Kaufman
[
GK22
, Appendix A]
. This technique did not appear as a theorem, rather it was used to show cover-testability of a certain complex (referred there as the representation complex). We observe that their argument can be viewed equivalently as coboundary expansion of another complex, related to the one they wish to analyze. Their technique can be generalized to show lower bounds of many other situations.

Let us describe the essence of this this technique informally. Let
X
be a two dimensional simplicial complex that we wish to show is a coboundary expander. Suppose that we can decompose
X
into many sub-complexes that are coboundary expanders. That is, let
Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{m}\subseteq X
be sub complexes such that
X=Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}\cup\dots\cup Y_{m}
, and assume that every
Y_{i}
is a coboundary expander. What can we ask from this decomposition, so that it will imply that
X
itself will be an coboundary expander?

For concreteness let us fix our group of coefficients to be
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. Let
f:X(1)\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
be such that
{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)=\varepsilon
. As a first attempt, we can look at the restrictions
\{f|_{Y_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{m}
and using the coboundary expansion of the
Y_{i}
’s separately we find
g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{m}
, where
g_{i}:Y_{i}(0)\to\{0,1\}
is such that

\beta\dist(f|_{Y_{i}},{\delta}g_{i})\leqslant{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f|_{Y_{i}}).

This is not enough though, since our goal is to find a single function
g:X(0)\to\{0,1\}
such that

\beta^{\prime}\dist(f,g)\leqslant{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f),

for some
\beta^{\prime}
that depends on
\beta
. The problem is that possibly some of the
Y_{i}
’s intersect, and it could be the case that
v\in Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}
is such that
g_{i}(v)\neq g_{j}(v)
. To model this problem we consider the
agreement graph
.

At this point let us assume for simplicity that the intersection between every two
Y_{i}
’s has at most one element. The agreement graph
\mathcal{A}
is the graph whose vertices are the
Y_{i}
’s and
Y_{i}\sim Y_{j}
if and only if
Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}\neq\emptyset
.
We also include two dimensional faces in
Y
, by putting
\{u,v,w\}\in Y(2)
whenever the three edges
uv,vw,uw
are in the graph.

To continue modeling the problem, let us define the
agreement function

h:\mathcal{A}(1)\to\{0,1\}
, such that
h(Y_{i},Y_{j})=g_{i}(v)-g_{j}(v)
where
v\in Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}
.
Note that
h
depends on
v
, and will later really be a function of the labeled edge
\{Y_{i},Y_{j}\}_{v}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Figure 3:
A triangle in
X
, in
\mathcal{A}
, and in the GK decomposition

A priori, we would like
h=0
on every edge of
\mathcal{A}
, or at least
h\approx 0
. However, Gotlib and Kaufman noticed that it is enough to require that
h
is close to a coboundary, i.e.
h\approx{\delta}\ell
for some
\ell:\mathcal{A}(0)\to\{0,1\}
. The reason is that if we define
\tilde{g}_{i}:Y_{i}(0)\to\{0,1\}
by
\tilde{g}_{i}(v)=g_{i}(v)+\ell(Y_{i})
then since
\ell(Y_{i})
is constant (the input is the vertex
v
, not
Y_{i}
), it holds that
{\delta}\tilde{g}_{i}={\delta}g_{i}
. Moreover, it holds that
\tilde{g}_{i}(v)=\tilde{g}_{j}(v)
if and only if
g_{i}(v)+g_{j}(v)=\ell(i)+\ell(j)
, if and only if
h(Y_{i},Y_{j})={\delta}\ell(Y_{i},Y_{j})
. In other words,

\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\neq\tilde{g}_{j}(v)\;\Leftrightarrow\;h(Y_{i},Y_{j})\neq{\delta}\ell(Y_{i},Y_{j}).

(5.1)

Hence let
\ell:\mathcal{A}(0)\to\{0,1\}
be such that
\dist(h,\ell)=\varepsilon^{\prime}
is as small as possible. We can use these
\{\tilde{g}_{i}\}
to define a single function via majority
g(v)=\maj_{v\in Y_{i}}\{\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\}
.

Let us see how we can bound
\dist(f,{\delta}g)
in terms of
{\mathrm{wt}}(f)
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\dist(f,{\delta}g)

\displaystyle=\E_{{i}}\left[\dist(f|_{Y_{i}},{\delta}\tilde{g}_{i})+\dist({\delta}\tilde{g}_{i},{\delta}\tilde{g})\right]

(5.2)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\beta}{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)+\E_{{i}}\left[\dist({\delta}\tilde{g}_{i},{\delta}g)\right],

(5.3)

where the inequality is due to
\beta
-coboundary expansion of every
Y_{i}
(and taking expectation). On the other hand, by expansion arguments we can argue that

\E_{{i}}\left[\dist({\delta}\tilde{g}_{i},{\delta}g)\right]\leqslant 2\E_{{i}}\left[\dist(\tilde{g}_{i},g)\right]=2\E_{{v}}\left[\ProbOp_{{i}}\left[g_{i}(v)\neq g(v)\right]\right].

where the inequality is just by a union bound (since if
\tilde{g}_{i},g
agree on both end points of an edge they also agree on the edge). The probability of
\tilde{g}_{i}
agreeing with
g
is bounded by
\ProbOp_{{i,j}}\left[\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\neq\tilde{g}_{j}(v)\right]=\ProbOp_{{i,j}}\left[h(Y_{i},Y_{j})\neq\ell(Y_{i},Y_{j})\right]
by (
5.1
). Hence

\eqref{eq:dist-f-maj-cob}\leqslant\frac{1}{\beta}{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)+2\dist(h,{\delta}\ell)=\frac{1}{\beta}{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)+2\varepsilon^{\prime}.

(5.4)

It looks like we’ve made some progress, but we are still missing a crucial component. How do we bound
\varepsilon^{\prime}
? So far we haven’t required anything yet from the structure of the agreement graph. To continue bounding
\varepsilon^{\prime}
we require that:

1.

This agreement graph is (a skeleton of) a
2
-dimensional agreement complex
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

This complex is a
\beta^{\prime}
-coboundary expander.

3.

Moreover, we can sample a triangle
\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}\in\mathcal{A}(2)
by sampling
uvw\in X(2)
and then sampling a triangle
\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}
such that
u\in Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}
,
v\in Y_{j}\cap Y_{k}
and
w\in Y_{k}\cap Y_{i}
.

If this is the case then it holds that

\varepsilon^{\prime}=\dist(h,\ell)\leqslant\frac{3+\beta}{\beta\beta^{\prime}}{\mathrm{wt}}(f).

(5.5)

The reason is as follows. For a triangle
\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}
we denote by
\{u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki}\}\in X(2)
the triangle such that
u_{ij}\in Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}
,
u_{jk}\in Y_{j}\cap Y_{k}
and
u_{ki}\in Y_{k}\cap Y_{i}
. By coboundary expansion of
\mathcal{A}
, it holds that
\dist(h,\ell)\leqslant\frac{1}{\beta^{\prime}}{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}h)
, so we will actually try to bound
{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}h)
.

For a triangle
\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}

{\delta}h=0
if
{\delta}f(u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki})=0
and for all three edges
e
of the triangle
\{u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki}\}
it holds that
f(e)={\delta}g_{m}(e)
(for
m=i,j,k
is the
Y_{m}
that contains the edge). The reason is that

\displaystyle{\delta}h(Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{K})

\displaystyle=h(Y_{i},Y_{j})+h(Y_{j},Y_{k})+h(Y_{k},Y_{i})

\displaystyle=(g_{i}(u_{ij})+g_{j}(u_{ij}))+(g_{j}(u_{jk})+g_{k}(u_{jk}))+(g_{k}(u_{ki})+g_{i}(u_{ki}))

\displaystyle=(g_{i}(u_{ij})+g_{i}(u_{ik}))+(g_{j}(u_{ij})+g_{j}(u_{jk}))+(g_{k}(u_{jk})+g_{k}(u_{ik}))

\displaystyle={\delta}g_{i}(u_{ij},u_{ik})+{\delta}g_{j}(u_{ij},u_{jk})+{\delta}g_{k}(u_{ik},u_{jk})

\displaystyle\overset{f={\delta}g_{m}}{=}f(u_{ij},u_{ik})+f(u_{ij},u_{jk})+f(u_{ik},u_{jk})

\displaystyle={\delta}f(u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki})

\displaystyle\overset{{\delta}f=0}{=}0.

Thus
{\mathrm{wt}}(h)\leqslant 3\E_{{i}}\left[\dist(f|_{Y_{i}},{\delta}{g}_{i})\right]+{\mathrm{wt}}(f)\leqslant\left(\frac{3}{\beta}+1\right){\mathrm{wt}}(f)
and (
5.5
) follows. Plugging this back in (
5.4
) we get that

\dist(f,{\delta}g)\leqslant\Omega(1/\beta\beta^{\prime}){\mathrm{wt}}(f).

5.1
Technical aspects of the theorem

Let us go into some more technical weeds of the theorem.

The most significant deviation from the overview (and from Gotlib and Kaufman’s scope) is that we no longer require
\left\lvert Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}\right\rvert\leqslant 1
, but instead study agreement graphs and complexes with multi edges. To our knowledge coboundary and cosystolic expansion of complexes with multi edges were not studied, but the definitions extend naturally to this case as well. In
Section
6
we show a useful reduction that lower bounds coboundary expansion of multi edged complexes, using the coboundary expansion of their single edged counterpart, provided the multi edges have some expanding structure.

Another significant difference is that in the technical overview we assumed that the triangle distribution
\mu_{2}
of the simplicial complex
X
in defined by choosing
Y_{i}
and then choosing a triangle. Moreover, it was assumed that this same of triangles in
X
is used to sample a triangle in the agreement complex
\mathcal{A}
. I.e. that sampling a triangle
\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}\in\mathcal{A}(2)
by sampling
uvw\in X(2)
and then sampling a triangle
\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}
such that
u\in Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}
,
v\in Y_{j}\cap Y_{k}
and
w\in Y_{k}\cap Y_{i}
.

It turns out that this is this assumption in too rigid to be of use in practice. For example, in
[
GK22
]
, the triangles were partitioned to two sets
X(2)=T_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}T_{2}
of relative size
\frac{1}{2}
. The triangles in one set
T_{1}
were used for the first step, where we fixed
f
locally to
g_{i}
for every
Y_{i}
. The other set
T_{2}
, was used to test agreement in the agreement complex. This set of triangles was referred to as “empty triangles” in
[
GK22
]
.

Hence we will compare three distributions: the actual distribution
\mu_{2}
on the triangles of
X
, the distribution
\nu
used to locally correct
f
to
g_{i}
in every
Y_{i}
, and the distribution
\pi
used to sample triangles in the agreement complex. Our actual requirement will be that marginals of these distributions will be smooth with respect to one another as in
Definition
2.1
.

5.2
Definitions for this theorem

Recall that for a simplicial complex with multi-edges
\{u,v\}_{i}
, we denote a triangle
\{u,v,w\}_{i,j,k}
to be the triangle whose vertices are
u,v,w
and whose edges are
\{u,v\}_{i},\{v,w\}_{j},\{w,u\}_{k}
.

Definition 5.1

(agreement complex)
.

Let
X
be a
2
-dimensional simplicial complex and let
\mathcal{Y}=\{Y_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a family of sub-complexes of
X
. A complex
X
is a agreement complex with respect to
X,\mathcal{Y}
if,

1.

\mathcal{A}(0)=\mathcal{Y}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}(1)\subseteq\left\{\{Y_{i},Y_{j}\}_{v}\left|\;\vphantom{\{Y_{i},Y_{j}\}_{v}v\in Y_{i}(0)\cap Y_{j}(0)}\right.v\in Y_{i}(0)\cap Y_{j}(0)\right\}
.

3.

\mathcal{A}(2)\subseteq\left\{\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}_{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}}\left|\;\vphantom{\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}_{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}}\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}\in X(2)}\right.\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}\in X(2)\right\}
.

Definition 5.2

(GK-decomposition)
.

A GK-decomposition of
X
is a tuple
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\nu,\pi)
such that

1.

\mathcal{Y}=\{Y_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, are such that
Y_{i}\subseteq X
are pure sub complexes.

2.

\mathcal{A}
is an agreement complex with respect to
\mathcal{Y}
whose distribution over triangles is
\pi
.

3.

\nu
is a distribution over tuples
(\{u,v,w\},Y_{i})
such that
\{u,v,w\}\in Y_{i}
.

For a distribution
\nu
and
i=0,1,2
we define
\nu_{i}
to be the marginal that just outputs the
i
-face chosen by
\nu
. E.g. for
i=1
we just take a random edge inside the triangle in the tuple chosen. We also denote by
\nu_{i,y}
the marginal that outputs the
i
-face chosen by
\nu
along with the sub complex
Y_{i}
. Furthermore, we denote by
\nu_{y}
the marginal distribution of
\nu
over
\mathcal{Y}
. Finally, we denote by
\nu|_{Y_{i}}
to be
\nu
conditioned on
Y_{i}
being the chosen subcomplex.

For a distribution
\pi
supported over
\mathcal{A}(2)
we also need similar notation. Let
i=0,1,2
. The distribution
\pi_{i}
is the marginal distribution that outputs a
i
-face that is a sub face of the labels of the chosen triangle. E.g. for
i=1
, if
\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}_{u,v,w}\sim\pi
then
\pi_{1}
outputs one of three edges
uv,vw,uw\in X(1)
. For
i=1
we denote by
\pi_{1,y}
the distribution that outputs
(uv,Y_{j})
. That is, if
\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}_{u,v,w}\sim\pi
, then
\pi_{1,y}
outputs a random edge in
\{u,v,w\}
, along with the
Y_{j}
such that
\{Y_{i},Y_{j}\}_{u},\{Y_{j},Y_{k}\}_{v}
participate in the triangle. Finally,
\pi_{0,y}
is the marginal of
\pi_{1,y}
that samples one vertex of the sampled edge uniformly at random. I.e. if
(uv,Y_{j})\sim\pi_{1,y}
then
\pi_{0,y}
samples either
(u,Y_{j})
or
(v,Y_{j})
.

Definition 5.3

(Local graph)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an agreement complex as above. For every
v\in\mathcal{A}(0)
the local graph
\mathcal{A}^{v}
is a graph whose vertices are all
Y_{i}\ni v
. The edges are all
Y_{i}\sim Y_{j}
such that
Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}\ni v
, where we choose an edge according to the distribution of the agreement complex, given that it was labeled by
v
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
X
be a
2
-dimensional simplicial complex and let
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\nu,\pi)
be a GK-decomposition. Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\eta>0
. Assume that the following holds.

1.

Every
Y_{i}
is a coboundary expander with
h^{1}(Y_{i})\geqslant\beta
, with respect to the distribution
\nu|_{Y_{i}}
.

2.

h^{1}(\mathcal{A})\geqslant\gamma
.

3.

Let
A\subseteq X(0)
be the set of vertices that are contained in at least two
Y_{i}
’s. For every
v\in A
, the local graph
\mathcal{A}^{v}
is a
\eta
-edge expander.

4.

Recall that
\mu_{i}
is the distribution of
i
-faces in
X
. Then the following relations between distributions hold:

(a)

(\nu_{2},\mu_{2})
and
(\pi_{2},\mu_{2})
are
\alpha
-smooth
3
3

3

as in
Definition
2.1
.
.

(b)

(\mu_{1},\nu_{1})
are
\alpha
-smooth.

(c)

(\nu_{0,y},\pi_{0,y})
are
(A_{Y},\alpha)
-smooth. Here
A_{Y}
are all
(v,Y_{i})
such that
v\in Y_{i}
and
v\in A
.

(d)

(\pi_{1,y},\nu_{1,y})
are
\alpha
-smooth.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\penalty\ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\penalty\ \nu\penalty\ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\penalty\ \mu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(2)\hbox{ and }\mathcal{A}(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\circ(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

Figure 4
:
\pi
is the joint distribution over
X\times\mathcal{A}
;
\nu
is the distribution describing the decomposition of
X
into
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m}
; and
\mu
is the distribution over
X
.

Then
X
is a coboundary expander and
h^{1}(X)\geqslant\frac{\alpha^{4}\beta\gamma\eta}{10}
.

The relations between
\pi,\nu,\mu
are schematically shown in
Figure
4
. The conditions on the distributions quantify the quality of the decomposition. Note that in case
\alpha=1
the distributions
\mu
and
\nu
are obtained as marginals of the distribution
\pi
. In this case
\mu_{i}=\nu_{i}
. The relation between
\nu
and
\pi
says that the global distribution on
\mathcal{A}
is compatible with the localized pieces (described by
\nu
).

We also encourage the readers to go over examples of where this theorem is used to see examples of decompositions that satisfy these items (such as
Proposition
8.7.1
, and also
Claim
2.8.2
,
Lemma
8.6
and
Lemma
8.9
), so that one will observe that these are easy to check in “practical” use cases.

Proof of
Theorem
5.4
.

Fix any group
\Gamma
. Let
f\in C^{1}(X,\Gamma)
be such that
{\mathrm{wt}}(f)=\varepsilon
, and we need to find some
g:X(0)\to\Gamma
such that
\dist(f,{\delta}g)\leqslant\frac{10}{\alpha^{4}\beta\gamma\eta}\varepsilon
. In particular by
Claim
2.1.1
, and by the fact that
(\nu_{2},\mu_{2})
are
\alpha
-smooth,

\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim\nu_{2}}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq 0\right]\leqslant\alpha^{-1}\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim\mu_{2}}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq 0\right]=\alpha^{-1}\varepsilon.

Let
{\delta}g_{i}\in B^{1}(Y_{i},\Gamma)
be such that
\dist(f|_{Y_{i}},{\delta}g_{i})
is minimal. By coboundary expansion of every
Y_{i}
,

\dist(f|_{Y_{i}},{\delta}g_{i})\leqslant\beta^{-1}\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim\nu|_{Y_{i}}}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq 0\right].

So in particular

\displaystyle\E_{{Y_{i}\sim\nu_{y}}}\left[\dist(f|_{Y_{i}},{\delta}g_{i})\right]\leqslant\beta^{-1}\E_{{Y_{i}\sim\nu_{y}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim\nu_{Y_{i}}}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq 0\right]\right]=\beta^{-1}\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim\nu_{2}}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq 0\right]\leqslant\alpha^{-1}\beta^{-1}\varepsilon.

(5.6)

We now turn to defining the correction function
{\delta}g
of
f
. Denote by
h:\overset{\to}{\mathcal{A}}(1)\to\Gamma
the agreement function i.e.

h((Y_{i},Y_{j})_{v})=g_{i}(v)^{-1}g_{j}(v).

Let
{\delta}\ell:\overset{\to}{\mathcal{A}}(1)\to\Gamma
be a coboundary closest to
h
(chosen arbitrarily). Let
\tilde{g}_{i}
be the functions
\tilde{g}_{i}(v)=g_{i}(v)\ell(Y_{i})
. It is easy to check that

{\delta}g_{i}={\delta}\tilde{g}_{i}

(5.7)

More interestingly,

\tilde{g}_{i}(v)=\tilde{g}_{j}(v)\quad\hbox{if and only if}\quad h((Y_{i},Y_{j})_{v})={\delta}\ell((Y_{i},Y_{j})_{v}).

(5.8)

The reason is that
\tilde{g}_{i}(v)=\tilde{g}_{j}(v)

can be expanded to

g_{i}(v)\ell(Y_{i})=g_{j}(v)\ell(Y_{j})

which, by multiplying by
\ell(Y_{j})^{-1}
on the right and
g_{i}(v)^{-1}
on the left, is equivalent to

\ell(Y_{i},Y_{j})=\ell(Y_{i})\ell(Y_{j})^{-1}=g_{i}(v)^{-1}g_{j}(v)=h((Y_{i},Y_{j})_{v}).

We finally define
g:X(0)\to\Gamma
by
g(v)=\maj_{Y_{i}\ni v}\{\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\}
where the most popular assignment is with respect to
\pi_{0,y}
, conditioned on
v
being the chosen vertex.
Let us analyze the distance of
{\delta}g
to
f
.

\displaystyle\dist(f,{\delta}g)

\displaystyle\leqslant\alpha^{-1}\ProbOp_{{uv\sim\nu|_{X(1)}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right]=\alpha^{-1}\E_{{Y_{i}\sim\nu_{Y}}}\left[\dist_{Y_{i}}(f|_{Y_{i}},{\delta}g)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\alpha^{-1}\left(\E_{{Y_{i}\sim\nu_{Y}}}\left[\dist_{Y_{i}}(f|_{Y_{i}},{\delta}\tilde{g}_{i})\right]+\E_{{Y_{i}\sim\nu_{Y}}}\left[\dist_{Y_{i}}({\delta}\tilde{g}_{i},{\delta}g)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\alpha^{-1}\left(\E_{{Y_{i}\sim\nu_{Y}}}\left[\dist_{Y_{i}}(f|_{Y_{i}},{\delta}\tilde{g}_{i})\right]+2\E_{{Y_{i}\sim\nu_{Y}}}\left[\dist_{Y_{i}}(\tilde{g}_{i},g)\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}\left(\E_{{Y_{i}\sim\nu_{Y}}}\left[\dist_{Y_{i}}(f|_{Y_{i}},{\delta}g_{i})\right]+2\ProbOp_{{(Y_{i},v)\sim\nu_{0,y}}}\left[\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\neq g(v)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\alpha^{-2}\beta^{-1}\varepsilon+2\alpha^{-1}\ProbOp_{{(Y_{i},v)\sim\nu_{0,y}}}\left[\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\neq g(v)\right].

Here the first inequality is by
\alpha
-smoothness of
(\mu_{1},\nu_{1})
. The second inequality follows from the triangle inequality and the third inequality is by the fact that if
\tilde{g}_{i}
and
g
are equal on both vertices of an edge, then
{\delta}\tilde{g}_{i}
and
{\delta}g
are equal on that edge. The last equality is because
{\delta}\tilde{g}_{i}={\delta}g_{i}
and by definition of the expectation over distance, and the last inequality is by (
5.6
). Altogether,

\displaystyle\dist(f,{\delta}g)

\displaystyle\leqslant\alpha^{-2}\beta^{-1}\varepsilon+2\alpha^{-1}\ProbOp_{{(Y_{i},v)\sim\nu_{0,y}}}\left[\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\neq g(v)\right].

(5.9)

We move on to bound
\ProbOp_{{(Y_{i},v)\sim\nu_{0,y}}}\left[\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\neq g(v)\right]
.

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{(Y_{i},v)\sim\nu_{0,y}}}\left[\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\neq g(v)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{\leqslant}\alpha^{-1}\ProbOp_{{(v,Y_{i})\sim\pi_{0,y}}}\left[\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\neq g(v)\right]

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}\E_{{v}}\left[\ProbOp_{{Y_{i}\in\mathcal{A}^{v}(0)}}\left[\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\neq g(v)\right]\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(2)}{\leqslant}\eta^{-1}\alpha^{-1}\E_{{v}}\left[\ProbOp_{{\{Y_{i},Y_{j}\}\sim\mathcal{A}^{v}(1)}}\left[\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\neq\tilde{g}_{j}(v)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}\eta^{-1}\ProbOp_{{\{Y_{i},Y_{j}\}_{v}\sim\mathcal{A}(1)}}\left[\tilde{g}_{i}(v)\neq\tilde{g}_{j}(v)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:g_and_H}}{=}\alpha^{-1}\eta^{-1}\ProbOp_{{\{Y_{i},Y_{j}\}_{v}\sim\mathcal{A}(1)}}\left[h((Y_{i},Y_{j})_{v})\neq{\delta}\ell((Y_{i},Y_{j})_{v})\right]

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}\eta^{-1}\dist(h,{\delta}\ell)

\displaystyle\overset{(3)}{\leqslant}\alpha^{-1}\gamma^{-1}\eta^{-1}wt({\delta}h).

Here
(1)
is because for every vertex, if
v\notin A
then
\tilde{g}_{i}(v)=g(v)
, hence the event is contained in
A_{Y}
and we can use
(A_{Y},\alpha)
-smoothness and with
Claim
2.1.1
. The next inequality
(2)
is by
Claim
2.3.1
and the fact that every local graph
\mathcal{A}^{v}
is a
\eta
-expander.
(3)
comes from
\gamma
-coboundary expansion of
\mathcal{A}
.

We conclude the proof by showing that

wt({\delta}h)\leqslant\alpha^{-1}\varepsilon+3\alpha^{-2}\beta^{-1}\varepsilon
.

Let us first show that if
{\delta}h(\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}_{u,v,w})\neq 0
then either
{\delta}f(uvw)\neq 0
or for one of the three edges
f(xy)\neq{\delta}g_{m}(xy)
(where
xy
in one of the edges in
uvw
and
Y_{m}
is the sub-complex that contains this edge, that was selected in the triangle). Otherwise,

\displaystyle{\delta}h(\{Y_{i},Y_{j},Y_{k}\}_{u,v,w})

\displaystyle=h((Y_{i},Y_{j})_{u})\cdot h((Y_{j},Y_{k})_{v})\cdot h((Y_{k},Y_{i})_{w})

\displaystyle=(g_{i}(u)^{-1}g_{j}(u))\cdot(g_{j}(v)^{-1}g_{k}(v))\cdot(g_{k}(w)^{-1}g_{i}(w))

\displaystyle=(g_{i}(w)^{-1}g_{i}(w))\cdot(g_{i}(u)^{-1}g_{j}(u))\cdot(g_{j}(v)^{-1}g_{k}(v))\cdot(g_{k}(w)^{-1}g_{i}(w))

\displaystyle\overset{f={\delta}g}{=}g_{i}(w)^{-1}f(uv)f(vw)f(wu)g_{i}(w)

\displaystyle=g_{i}(w)^{-1}{\delta}f(uvw)g_{i}(w)

\displaystyle\overset{{\delta}f=Id}{=}Id.

Thus

{\mathrm{wt}}(h)\leqslant\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim\pi_{2}}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq 0\right]+3\ProbOp_{{(uv,Y_{i})\sim\pi_{1,y}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g_{i}(uv)\right].

By
\alpha
-smoothness of
(\pi_{2},\mu_{2})
it holds that
\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim\pi_{2}}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq 0\right]\leqslant\alpha^{-1}\varepsilon
.
By smoothness
\alpha
-smoothness of
(\pi_{1,y},\nu_{1,y})
, the rightmost term is bounded by

3\ProbOp_{{(uv,Y_{i})\sim\pi_{1,y}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g_{i}(uv)\right]\leqslant 3\alpha^{-1}\ProbOp_{{(uv,Y_{i})\sim\nu_{1,y}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g_{i}(uv)\right]=3\alpha^{-1}\E_{{Y_{i}\sim\nu_{y}}}\left[\dist(f|_{Y_{i}},{\delta}g_{i})\right].

By (
5.6
) we bound this by
3\alpha^{-2}\beta^{-1}\varepsilon
. Combining the above yields
{\mathrm{wt}}(h)\leqslant\alpha^{-1}\varepsilon+3\alpha^{-2}\beta^{-1}\varepsilon
.

Putting things back together we have

\displaystyle\dist(f,{\delta}g)

\displaystyle\leqslant\alpha^{-2}\beta^{-1}\varepsilon+2\alpha^{-2}\gamma^{-1}\eta^{-1}{\mathrm{wt}}(h)

(5.10)

\displaystyle\leqslant\alpha^{-1}\beta^{-1}\varepsilon+2\alpha^{-2}\gamma^{-1}\eta^{-1}\left(\alpha^{-1}+3\alpha^{-2}\beta^{-1}\right)\varepsilon

(5.11)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{10}{\alpha^{4}\beta\gamma\eta}\varepsilon.

(5.12)

∎

Remark 5.5
.

We could have made this theorem tighter by accounting for different smoothness parameters
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots
instead of a single
\alpha
. However, in all our examples this tightening would not have gained a significant improvement. We stated the theorem in this generality to keep it simpler.

6
Blow-ups of simplicial complexes

We saw above that analyzing coboundary expansion via the GK decomposition naturally gives rise to simplicial complexes with multi-edges. We show under mild conditions that coboundary expansion of a simplicial complex with multi-edges reduces to the coboundary expansion of its non-multi-edge “flattening”.

Recall that we denote by
\{u,v\}_{i}
an edge between vertices
u
and
v
that has label
i
. We also denote by

\{u,v,w\}_{i,j,k}
a triangle with vertices
u,v,w
and edges
\{u,v\}_{i},\{v,w\}_{j}
and
\{w,u\}_{k}
.

Let us begin with a definition of a blow-up graph. Let
G,G^{\prime}
be two graphs on the same vertex set
V
. We say that
G^{\prime}
is a blow-up of
G
if
G
is a simple graph (that has no multi edges) and
\{u,v\}\in E(G)
if and only if there exists an edge
\{u,v\}_{i}\in E(G^{\prime})
, and moreover, for every
\{u,v\}\in G
, the total weight of edges from
u
to
v
in
G^{\prime}
equals the weight of
\{u,v\}
in
G
,

\ProbOp_{{G}}\left[\{u,v\}\right]=\sum_{{i}:\,{\{u,v\}_{i}\in E(G^{\prime})}}\ProbOp_{{G^{\prime}}}\left[\{u,v\}_{i}\right].

Similarly, for simplicial complexes,

Definition 6.1

(Blow-up)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-dimensional simplicial complex. Let
\tilde{X}
be a simplicial complex with multi-edges. We say that
\tilde{X}
is an
blow-up
of
X
if
\tilde{X}(0)=X(0)
and such that for every face
s\in X
, the probability of sampling a face
\tilde{s}\in\tilde{X}
whose vertices are
s
, is the probability of sampling
s\in X
.

Definition 6.2

(Label graph)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-dimensional simplicial complex and
\tilde{X}
be a blow-up of
X
. For a fixed edge
e=\{u,v\}\in X(1)
let
the label graph

G^{e}=G^{e}(\tilde{X})
be the graph whose vertices are all labels of labeled edges in
\tilde{X}
between
u
and
v
, i.e.

V(G^{e})=\{i:\{uv\}_{i}\in\tilde{X}\}.

In order to define the edges we first consider a bipartite graph that connects a label
i
to a labeled triangle
\{u,v,w\}_{i,j,k}
that contains
\{u,v\}_{i}
. We then let the label graph of the edge
\{u,v\}
be the two step walk on this bipartite graph. In other words,
i
is connected to
{i^{\prime}}
if there are
w,j,k
such that the completions
\{u,v,w\}_{i,j,k}
and
\{u,v,w\}_{i^{\prime},j,k}
exist.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
X
be a
\beta
-coboundary expander with respect to coefficients
\Gamma
. Let
\tilde{X}
be a blow-up of
X
such that for every
e=uv\in X(1)
,
G^{e}
is an
\eta
-edge expander. Then
\tilde{X}
is a
\frac{1}{5}\eta\beta
-coboundary expander with respect to coefficients
\Gamma
.

Let us give some intuition for
Lemma
6.3
in case
\Gamma=\mathbb{F}_{2}
. Fix
h\in C^{1}(\tilde{X},\mathbb{F}_{2})
, and assume
{\delta}h=0
. Every triangle
t=\{u,v,w\}_{i,k,\ell}\in\tilde{X}(2)
defines an equation

{\delta}h(t)=h(\{u,v\}_{i})+h(\{v,w\}_{j})+h(\{w,u\}_{k})=0.

(6.1)

Let
EQ
be the set of all such equations (i.e. the solution space of
EQ
is
Z^{1}(\tilde{X},\mathbb{F}_{2})
). As a warm up, we want to make sure that
Z^{1}(\tilde{X},\mathbb{F}_{2})=B^{1}(\tilde{X},\mathbb{F}_{2})
, i.e. that
Z^{1}(\tilde{X},\mathbb{F}_{2})
is the set of all
h:\tilde{X}(1)\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
such that there exists some
g:\tilde{X}(0)\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
such that

h(\{u,v\}_{i})=g(u)+g(v).

In particular, we note that for any such
h
,
h(\{u,v\}_{i})
is independent of the label
i
, or in other words for every
i,j
,

h(\{u,v\}_{i})=h(\{u,v\}_{j}).

We learn from this that a necessary condition for
\tilde{X}
to have
Z^{1}(\tilde{X},\mathbb{F}_{2})=B^{1}(\tilde{X},\mathbb{F}_{2})
, is that the equations
h(\{u,v\}_{i})=h(\{u,v\}_{j})
are spanned by
EQ
. For a fixed edge
\{u,v\}\in X
and two of its labels
i,j
, if there are two triangles
\{u,v,w\}_{i,k,\ell},\{u,v,w\}_{j,k,\ell}\in\tilde{X}
, then by adding up their two corresponding equations as in (
6.1
) yields

h(\{u,v\}_{i})=h(\{u,v\}_{j}).

Moreover, one can observe that if
G^{\{u,v\}}
is connected, then all the equations

h(\{u,v\}_{i})=h(\{u,v\}_{j})

are spanned by
EQ
. The fact that
Z^{1}(\tilde{X},\mathbb{F}_{2})=B^{1}(\tilde{X},\mathbb{F}_{2})
is not enough to lower bound coboundary expansion. However, this hints that a robust notion of connectivity for the
G^{\{u,v\}}
, may be useful for proving such a lower bound.

Indeed, Suppose that
h(\{u,v\}_{i})=h(\{u,v\}_{j})
is violated for many labels
i,j
. Let
V_{0},V_{1}\subseteq V(G^{\{u,v\}})
be such that
i\in V_{x}
if
h(\{u,v\}_{i})=x
. If
h(\{u,v\}_{i})=h(\{u,v\}_{j})
is violated for many pairs of labels
i,j
that are not necessarily edges, then both
V_{0},V_{1}
are large. If both
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
are large, then by the expansion of
G^{\{u,v\}}
, many edges cross between
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
. Every such edge corresponds to two triangles
\{u,v,w\}_{i,k,\ell},\{u,v,w\}_{j,k,\ell}
. And because
h(\{u,v\}_{i})\neq h(\{u,v\}_{j})
it holds that
h(\{u,v\}_{i})+h(\{u,v\}_{j})=1
. On the other hand,
h(\{u,v\}_{i})+h(\{u,v\}_{j})
is the sum of equations

h(\{u,v\}_{i})+h(\{v,w\}_{k})+h(\{w,u\}_{\ell})=0

and

h(\{u,v\}_{j})+h(\{v,w\}_{k})+h(\{w,u\}_{\ell})=0

and if the sum is non zero, then at least one of these equations is violated.

The contra-positive argument is that if most of these equations sum up to zero, that is,

\ProbOp_{{w,i,k,\ell}}\left[{\delta}h(\{u,v,w\}_{i,k,\ell}=0\right]\approx 0,

then the cut
V_{0},V_{1}
must have few crossing edges. In an expander graph this implies that one of the sets is small, or in other words, that
i\mapsto h(\{u,v\}_{i})
is almost a constant. Thus defining the majority function
Mh:X(1)\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
,
Mh(\{u,v\})=\maj_{i}h(\{u,v\}_{i})
we get by the discussion above that

\ProbOp_{{\{u,v\}_{i}\in\tilde{X}(1)}}\left[h(\{u,v\}_{i})=Mh(\{u,v\})\right]\approx{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}h).

Now we use the coboundary expansion in
X
to correct
Mh
to some
{\delta}g\in B^{1}(X,\mathbb{F}_{2})
. This
g
will have the property that
\Omega(\beta)\dist(h,{\delta}g)\leqslant{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}h)
.

Proof of
Lemma
6.3
.

Let
h:\tilde{X}(1)\to\Gamma
be such that
wt({\delta}h)=\varepsilon
.
Let us define
Mh:\tilde{X}(1)\to\Gamma
to be
Mh(\{u,v\}_{j})=\maj_{i\in V(G^{\{u,v\}})}\{h(\{u,v\}_{i}\}
.
By the triangle inequality,

\dist(h,B^{1}(\tilde{X}))\leqslant\dist(h,Mh)+\dist(Mh,B^{1}(\tilde{X})).

(6.2)

The first term in the right hand side is bounded by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dist(h,Mh)&=\E_{{uv\in X(1)}}\left[\ProbOp_{{i}}\left[h(\{u,v\}_{i})\neq Mh(\{u,v\}_{i})\right]\right]\\
&\leqslant\eta^{-1}\E_{{uv\in X(1)}}\left[\ProbOp_{{i,j}}\left[h(\{u,v\}_{i})\neq h(\{u,v\}_{j})\right]\right]\\
&\leqslant\eta^{-1}\E_{{uv\in X(1)}}\left[2\ProbOp_{{\{u,v,w\}_{i,k,\ell}}}\left[{\delta}h(\{u,v,w\}_{i,k,\ell})\neq 0\right]\right]=2\eta^{-1}\varepsilon.\end{split}

(6.3)

where the first inequality follows from the definition of edge expansion, and the second inequality follows from the fact that if
h(\{u,v\}_{i})\neq h(\{u,v\}_{j})
then one of the triangles
\{u,v,w\}_{i,k,\ell},\{u,v,w\}_{j,k,\ell}
is not satisfied, since the only difference between the assignment of
h
to the edges of these two triangles is the assignment of
h(\{u,v\}_{i})
and
h(\{u,v\}_{j})
. The distribution of sampling
uv\in X(1)
and then a triangle
\{u,v,w\}_{i,j,k}
conditioned on
uv
is just the distribution over triangles in
\tilde{X}(2)
, hence the final equality with
2\eta^{-1}
times the weight of
{\delta}h
.

We turn to the second term on RHS of (
6.2
). First, we observe that

wt({\delta}Mh)\leqslant 3\ProbOp\left[{Mh\neq h}\right]+wt({\delta}h)\leqslant 3\eta^{-1}\varepsilon.

(6.4)

The reason is that we can split triangles into those where there is an edge with
Mh\neq h
and those where
Mh=h
on all three edges. The former are accounted for in the first term, and in the later case
{\delta}Mh(t)={\delta}h(t)
.

Since
Mh
does not depend on the label of an edge, it gives rise to
\bar{h}\in C^{1}(X)
defined by choosing for each edge
\{u,v\}
an arbitrary label
i
and setting
\bar{h}(\{u,v\})=Mh(\{u,v\}_{i})
. Clearly
\dist_{\tilde{X}}(Mh,B^{1}(\tilde{X}))=\dist_{X}(\bar{h},B^{1}(X))
and
wt_{X}({\delta}\bar{h})=wt_{\tilde{X}}({\delta}Mh)
so using the coboundary expansion of
X
,

\dist(Mh,B^{1}(\tilde{X}))=\dist(\bar{h},B^{1}(X))\leqslant\beta^{-1}wt({\delta}\bar{h})=\beta^{-1}wt({\delta}Mh)\leqslant 3\beta^{-1}\eta^{-1}\varepsilon.

(6.5)

where the last inequality comes from (
6.4
).
Plugging (
6.5
) and (
6.3
) into (
6.2
)
yields the result.
∎

7
General expansion of the faces complex

In this section we prove a general bound on the swap coboundary expansion of coboundary expanders.

Theorem

(Restatement of
Theorem
1.3
)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-dimensional simplicial complex. Let
r
be such that
7r+7\leqslant d
. Assume that for every
-1\leqslant m\leqslant r
and
s\in X(m)
,
h^{1}(X_{s})\geqslant\beta
and that
X
is a
\lambda
-two sided local spectral expander for
\lambda<\frac{1}{2r^{2}}
, then
X
is a
(\beta^{O(r)},r)
-swap coboundary expander.

The following corollary follows directly from
Theorem
2.15
.

Corollary 7.1
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional simplicial complex. Let
r
be such that
7r+8\leqslant n
. Assume that for every
-1<m\leqslant r
and
s\in X(m)
,
h^{1}(X_{s})\geqslant\beta
and that
X
is a
\lambda
-two sided local spectral for
\lambda\leqslant\exp(-O(r\log\beta))
, then
X
is a
(\exp(-O(r\log\beta)),r)
-swap cosystolic expander.

We note that a tighter analysis could perhaps lose the
O
inside the expression, which would perhaps allow us to get a sub exponential bound if one could show that most links have
h^{1}(X_{s}^{\{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}\}})\geqslant 1-o(1)
. However, as the state of the art is today, we do not know of such bounds in almost any complex and therefore we did not try to optimize the constant.

The bounds of
Theorem
1.3
can be improved for certain complexes. For example
Theorem
1.4
shows a better bound for spherical buildings with sufficiently large field size and dimension. For the complete complex we show, in the end of this section, a
constant
lower bound on the swap coboundary expansion.

We first prove
Theorem
1.3
for partite complexes (see
Proposition
7.1.1
). In
Section
7.1
we give a simpler exponential bound, and in
Section
7.2
we derive an improved bound. We then use a partitification reduction (in
Section
7.3
) to extend the proof to any complex.

7.1
Exponentially decaying coboundary expansion for partite complexes

In this section we prove the theorem for the important case of
X
being an
n
-partite complex.
Recall that for mutually disjoint sets of colors
J=\{c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{\ell}\}
the colored faces complex
\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X
is the
\ell
-partite complex whose vertices are
\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X(0)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}X[c_{i}]
and whose top-level faces are all
s=\{w_{1},\dots,w_{\ell}\}
such that
w_{i}\in X[c_{i}]
and
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}w_{i}\in X
.

Proposition 7.1.1
.

Let
X
be a
n
-partite complex that is a
\lambda
-local spectral expander for
\lambda\leqslant\frac{1}{2r^{2}}
. Let
\ell\geqslant 5
and let
J=\{c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{\ell}\}
be a set of mutually disjoint colors
c_{j}\subseteq[n]
,
\lvert c_{j}\rvert\leqslant r
. Denote by
R=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}|c_{j}|
. Let
\beta>0
and assume that for every
I=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\}
such that
i_{j}\in c_{j}
and every
w\in X^{\cup J\setminus I}
,
h^{1}(X_{w}^{I})\geqslant\beta
. Then
h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\beta_{1}^{R}
for
\beta_{1}=\Omega_{\ell}(\beta)
.

Proof.

Fix
\ell\geqslant 5
. The proof is via induction on
R
(for all partite complexes
X
simultaneously). The base cases are when either one of the
c_{i}=\emptyset
or when
|c_{1}|=|c_{2}|=\dots=|c_{\ell}|=1
. If one of the
c_{j}=\emptyset
then
h^{1}(X)=\Omega(1)
by
Claim
2.8.1
. Otherwise all the
c_{j}=\{i_{j}\}
’s are singletons. In this case
\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X\cong X^{\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\}}
and
h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\beta
by assumption.

Now let us assume that the proposition holds for
R
and prove it for
c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{\ell}
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}|c_{j}|=R+1
and all
c_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Fix such
c_{i}
’s.

We choose some
I=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\}
such that
i_{j}\in c_{j}
and show that

h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\Omega(h^{1}(X^{I}))\cdot\min_{j\in[\ell],v\in{X[i_{j}]}}h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J_{j}}\!X_{v})

(7.1)

where
J_{j}=\{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime},\dots,c_{\ell}^{\prime}\}
such that
c_{j}^{\prime}=c_{j}\setminus\{i_{j}\}
and for
m\neq j
,
c_{m}^{\prime}=c_{m}
. Observe that
\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}|c_{j}^{\prime}|=R
so if (
7.1
) holds then by induction
h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\Omega(\beta)\cdot\beta_{1}^{R}\geqslant\beta_{1}^{R+1}
. Indeed, the assumption that for every
I=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\}
such that
i_{j}\in c_{j}
and every
w\in X^{\cup J\setminus I}
,
h^{1}(X_{w}^{I})\geqslant\beta
implies that the same holds for
X_{v}
and the
c_{j}^{\prime}
’s, so we are justified to apply an inductive argument.

We show (
7.1
) by applying
Theorem
5.4
to the following GK-decomposition
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\nu,\pi)
.

1.

Let
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{Y_{v}\left|\;\vphantom{Y_{v}v\in X^{I}(0)}\right.v\in X^{I}(0)\right\}
, such that
Y_{v}\subseteq\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X
is the
\ell
partite complex induced by vertices that either contain
v
or are in
\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X(0)\cap X_{v}
.

2.

We define
\pi
so that
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}_{w,w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}}\sim\pi
is chosen as follows:

(a)

We sample
\{w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{\ell}\}\in\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X(\ell-1)
.

(b)

We sample a random triangle
t=\{w,w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}\}\subseteq\{w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{\ell}\}
.

(c)

We sample distinct
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\in I
and
t^{\prime}=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
to be such that
col(v_{j})=i_{j}
and such that every
v_{j}
is contained in one of the
w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{\ell}
that were sampled in the first step.

(d)

We randomly reorder and output
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}_{w,w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}}
.

We note that
\{w,w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}\}
is distributed by the triangle distribution of
X
,
\mu_{2,\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X}
.

3.

We define
\nu
to the a marginal of
\pi
. That is, we first sample
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}_{w,w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}}\sim\pi
and then take one of the three
v_{j}
’s and output
(Y_{v_{j}},\{w,w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}\})
.

4.

We identify the unlabeled triangles and edges of
\mathcal{A}
with those of
X^{I}
.

Let
v
be a vertex of color, say,
i_{1}\in c_{1}
. Then
Y_{v}\cong\mathrm{F}^{J_{1}}\!X_{v}
. This is because
Y_{v}[c_{1}]=\left\{\{v\}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w\left|\;\vphantom{\{v\}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}ww\in X_{v}[c_{1}^{\prime}]}\right.w\in X_{v}[c_{1}^{\prime}]\right\}
and for all
m\neq j
,
Y_{v}[c_{m}]=X_{v}[c_{m}]
. Moreover,
\nu
is defined such that the distribution of
\nu|_{Y_{v}}
is the distribution over triangles in
\mathrm{F}^{J_{1}}\!X_{v}
(up to the identification of
Y_{v}[c_{1}]\cong X_{v}[c_{1}^{\prime}]
).
It follows that

\min_{v}h^{1}(Y_{v})=\min_{j\in[\ell],v\in{X[i_{j}]}}h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J_{j}}\!X_{v}).

Before analyzing the agreement complex, let us consider the smoothness required in
Theorem
5.4
for this decomposition. For this we note that
\mu_{2,\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X}=\nu_{2}=\pi_{2}
and that
\mu_{1,\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X}=\nu_{1},\pi_{0,y}=\nu_{0,y},\pi_{1,y}=\nu_{1,y}
. Thus all pairs of distributions are
1
-smooth.

Now let us consider the local graphs of labels
w
of edges in
\mathcal{A}
. Let
w
be of color (say)
c_{1}
. The local graph
\mathcal{A}^{w}
is an
\ell
-partite graph. The first part is the (only)
v\in w
of dimension
i_{1}
. The rest of the parts are the vertices of colors
i_{2},i_{3},\dots,i_{\ell}
in
X_{w}(0)
. Two
v_{j},v_{j^{\prime}}
are connected if they belong to different parts and one of the following holds:

1.

col(v_{j})=1
.

2.

col(v_{j^{\prime}})=1
.

3.

\{v_{j},v_{j^{\prime}}\}\in X_{w}(1)
.

The edge distribution is by choosing two distinct
i_{j},i_{j^{\prime}}
and then choosing a uniform edge in the first two cases, or and edge in
X_{w}[\{i_{j},i_{j^{\prime}}\}]
in the third case.

This is a constant expander: Observe that there is a graph homomorphism between this graph and
K_{\ell}
(the complete graph over
\ell
vertices). Thus by
Claim
2.2.3
one needs to verify that for every
i_{j}\neq i_{j^{\prime}}
the bipartite graph induced by vertices of these colors is a bipartite expander. If
i_{j}=i_{1}
or
i_{j^{\prime}}=i_{1}
then this is a complete bipartite graph, otherwise this is the colored swap walk between
X_{w}[i_{j}]
to
X_{w}[i_{j^{\prime}}]
which is a
\lambda
-expander by assumption that
X
is a
\lambda
-local spectral.

It remains to lower bound the coboundary expansion of
\mathcal{A}
. As seen above, the agreement complex
\mathcal{A}
is a blow-up of
X^{I}
because every unlabeled triangle
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
is chosen with the same probability as in
X^{I}
.

Claim 7.1.1
.

Let
X
be a
\lambda<\frac{1}{2r^{2}}
local spectral expander. Then for every unlabeled edge
e_{0}=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in X^{I}(1)
, the label graph
G_{e_{0}}
is an
\Omega_{\ell}(1)
-edge expander.

We defer the proof of
Claim
7.1.1
to
Appendix
A
since it is a straightforward calculation. Believing this claim however we get, by
Lemma
6.3
, that
h^{1}(\mathcal{A})\geqslant\Omega(h^{1}(X^{I}))
.

By
Theorem
5.4
, it holds that
h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\Omega(h^{1}(\mathcal{A}))\cdot\min_{j,v}h^{1}(F^{J_{j}}X_{v})
. We fix
\beta_{1}=\Omega(\beta)
so that
h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\beta_{1}\cdot\min_{j,v}h^{1}(F^{J_{j}}X_{v})
and get by induction that
h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\beta_{1}^{R+1}
.
∎

7.2
Improved bound

In
Proposition
7.1.1
we show a bound of the form
h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\beta_{1}^{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}|c_{i}|}
where the base of the exponent was the worst coboundary expansion in any
\ell
-colored link of
X
. As we shall see in
Section
8
, sometimes this
\beta_{1}
is sub-constant. However, looking closely at the proof, we can observe that the worst case link expansion bound we use in the GK-decomposition can be replaced with a constant lower bound in all but
O(1)
of the induction steps.

To be more precise, in the inductive step we get to choose some
I=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\}
such that
i_{1}\in c_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}\in c_{\ell}
and using
Theorem
5.4
obtain a bound of the form

h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\underbrace{\Omega(h^{1}(X^{\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\}}))}_{A(X,I)}\cdot\underbrace{\min_{j\in[\ell],v\in X[i_{j}]}h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J_{j}}\!X_{v})}_{B(X,J,I)}

where
J_{j}=\{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime},\dots,c_{\ell}^{\prime}\}
is such that
c_{j}^{\prime}=c_{j}\setminus\{i_{j}\}
and
c_{m}^{\prime}=c_{m}
for
m\neq j
.

The first term
A(X,I)
is the term we bound directly. The second term
B(X,J,I)
is the one we bound using induction, by recursively doing more GK-decompositions. In
Proposition
7.1.1
we chose
I
arbitrarily and bounded
A(X,I)
by the worst possible expansion of
X^{I}
(and later inside the induction, this was bounded by the worst possible coboundary expansion of
X_{w}^{I}
for some link
X_{w}
).

The proof of
Proposition
7.1.1
shows that we can try to optimize over
I
, that is, the bound we actually get is

h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\max_{I}A(X,I)\cdot B(X,J,I).

(7.2)

In this case, the
A(X,I)=\Omega(h^{1}(X^{I}))
term is straightforward, but one needs to better understand what happens to the
B(X,J,I)
term when we go further down the induction.

One can understand this term using the perspective of a two-player game on a tree, as we explain here.

First let us describe the tree.

1.

The nodes of the tree correspond to
(J^{\prime},X_{w})
for
J^{\prime}\leqslant J
and
w\in X[\cup J\setminus\cup J^{\prime}]
.

2.

The leaves (i.e. basis of the induction) are all
(J^{\prime},X_{w})
so that either
J^{\prime}
contains an empty color, or so that all colors are singletons.

3.

The root is
(J,X)
(i.e.
X=X_{\emptyset}
, which is consistent with
\emptyset\in X[\cup J\setminus\cup J]
).

4.

For every non-leaf
(J^{\prime},X_{w_{1}})
, its children are the
(J^{\prime\prime},w_{2})
such that
J^{\prime\prime}\leqslant J^{\prime}
and
w_{2}=w_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{v\}
for some vertex
v
. (n particular this means that
|\cup J^{\prime\prime}|=|\cup J^{\prime}|-1
).

The two-player game is the following, where Player
1
tries to maximize a value
h
, and Player
2
tries to minimize it. The game begins at the root of the tree
(X,J)
with value
h=h_{0}=1
. At the first step, Player
1
chooses some
I\leqslant J
as above and ‘gains’
h^{1}(X^{I})
, i.e.
h_{1}=h_{0}\cdot h^{1}(X^{I})
. Then Player
2
chooses a child
(J_{j},X_{v})
such that
\cup J\setminus\cup J_{j}\in I
(this corrsponds to the minimum in
B(X,J,I)
. The two players traverse to
(J_{j},X_{v})
and the game continues. This process corresponds to the first step of the decomposition in (
7.2
) (for
X
).

In general, when we are at a node
(J^{\prime},X_{w})
, the first player chooses some
I=\{\{i_{1}\},\{i_{2}\},\dots,\{i_{\ell}\}\}\leqslant J^{\prime}
, and ‘gains’
h^{1}(X_{w}^{I})
(i.e.
h_{i+1}:=h_{i}\cdot h^{1}(X_{w}^{I})
). Then, if
(J^{\prime},X_{w})
is not a leaf, the second player chooses a child
(J^{\prime\prime},X_{w^{\prime}})
such that
\cup J^{\prime}\setminus\cup J^{\prime\prime}\in I
and the game continues on
(J^{\prime\prime},X_{w^{\prime}})
. If
(J^{\prime},X_{w})
is a leaf the game ends and its value is the current
h_{i+1}
. This step corresponds to (
7.2
), but for
X_{w}
instead of
X
. The maximal value of the game is the largest
\hat{h}
such that for any possible Player
2
, there exists a strategy of Player
1
that attains
h\geqslant\hat{h}
at the end of the game. Modifying
Proposition
7.1.1
can prove that
h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\hat{h}
.

Using this point of view let us see a sufficient condition on
X
that implies a better
\hat{h}
than the one obtained by the minimum.

For
J^{\prime}\leqslant J
let us denote by

d(J^{\prime})=\sum_{c_{j}^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}}|c_{j}^{\prime}|.

We observe that the
i
-th round of the game we are at a node
(J^{\prime},X_{w})
such that
d(J^{\prime})=R-i+1
.

Suppose that there are values
T_{q}
such that for every
(J^{\prime},X_{w})
with
d(J^{\prime})=q
, there exists a choice
I
for Player
1
so that they gain
h^{1}(X_{w}^{I})\geqslant T_{q}
. In every step in the gave
d(J^{\prime})
decreases by
1
, and it is never less than
1
. Thus it is easy to see that
\hat{h}\geqslant\prod_{q=1}^{R}T_{q}
.

Let us give a more mathematical description to this idea.
Let
q\leqslant R
be an integer.
\mathcal{J}_{q}=\mathcal{J}_{q}(J)
be all the
J^{\prime}=\{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime},\dots,c_{\ell}^{\prime}\}\leqslant J
such that
d(J^{\prime})=q
. Let

T_{q}(X,J)=\min_{(J^{\prime},X_{w}),J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{q},w\in X[\cup J\setminus\cup J^{\prime}]}\left(\max_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\text{ s.t. }i_{j}\in c_{j}^{\prime}}\left(h^{1}(X_{w}^{\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\}})\right)\right).

To state this explicitly, this is the largest
T_{q}
such that Player
1
is guaranteed to get when at a node
(J^{\prime},X_{w})
where
J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{q}(J)
.

The following proposition follows directly from the discussion above.

Proposition 7.2.1
.

Let
X
be a partite
\lambda
-one sided local spectral expander for
\lambda\leqslant\frac{1}{2r^{2}}
. Let
J=\{c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{\ell}\}
and let
R=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}|c_{j}|
. Then
h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\prod_{q=1}^{R}\Omega_{\ell}(T_{q}(X,J))
.

Proof.

In the proof of
Proposition
7.1.1
we showed (
7.1
). Now we show that (
7.1
) implies this proposition as well.

By definition
T_{R+1}(X,J)
is the minimum over the root only, i.e. we are only looking at
(X,J)
(since
J
is the only set with
d(J)=R+1
). Let
I=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\}
be such that
h^{1}(X^{I})
is maximized. Then
h^{1}(X^{I})\geqslant T_{R+1}(X,J)
.

By (
7.1
), we have that

h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J}\!X)\geqslant\Omega(h^{1}(X^{I}))\cdot\min_{j\in[\ell],v\in X[i_{j}]}(h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J_{j}}\!X_{v})\geqslant T_{R+1}(X,J))\cdot\min_{j\in[\ell],v\in X[i_{j}]}(h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{J_{j}}\!X_{v}).

since
h^{1}(X^{I})\geqslant T_{R+1}(X,J)
. By applying the induction hypothesis to the second term we get

\geqslant T_{R+1}(X,J)\cdot\min_{j\in[\ell],v\in X{[i_{j}]}}\prod_{q=1}^{R}T_{q}(X_{v},J_{j}).

Let us show that for every
j,v
in the minimum,

\prod_{q=1}^{R}T_{q}(X_{v},J_{j})\geqslant\prod_{q=1}^{R}T_{q}(X,J).

In fact we will show that for every
i_{j}
and vertex
v\in X[i_{j}]
,
T_{q}(X_{v},J_{j})\geqslant T_{q}(X,J)
.

The expression for
T_{q}(X_{v},J_{j})
is, by definition,

T_{q}(X_{v},J_{j})=\min_{(J^{\prime},X_{w}),J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{q}(J_{j}),w\in X_{v}[\cup J_{j}\setminus\cup J^{\prime}]}\left(\max_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\text{ s.t. }i_{j}\in c_{j}^{\prime}}\left(h^{1}(X_{v\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w}^{\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\}})\right)\right).

By making a change of variables
w^{\prime}=v\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w
and observing that
w\in X_{v}[\cup J_{j}\setminus\cup J^{\prime}]
if and only if
w^{\prime}\in X[\cup J\setminus\cup J^{\prime}]
, we can change this expression to

T_{q}(X_{v},J_{j})=\min_{(J^{\prime},X_{w^{\prime}}),J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{q}(J_{j}),v\in w^{\prime}\in X[\cup J\setminus\cup J^{\prime}]}\left(\max_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\text{ s.t. }i_{j}\in c_{j}^{\prime}}\left(h^{1}(X_{w^{\prime}}^{\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell}\}})\right)\right)

As we can see this is the same minimum as in
T_{q}(X,J)
, only that we consider less
J^{\prime}
(since
J^{\prime}\leqslant J_{j}
implies
J^{\prime}\leqslant J
) and less
w^{\prime}
(since in
T_{q}(X_{v},J_{j})
we require
v\in w^{\prime}
). Thus
T_{q}(X_{v},J_{j})\geqslant T_{q}(X,J)
and the proposition follows.
∎

7.3
Proof of
Theorem
1.3

Recall the definition of the partitification of a complex
X
in
Section
2
.

Proof of
Theorem
1.3
.

By
Claim
2.8.3

h^{1}(F^{r}X)=\Omega(h^{1}((F^{r}X)^{\dagger_{7}}))
so we show coboundary expansion of the
7
-partitification (not that the assumption on the dimensions of
X
imply that this complex exists).

For
i=1,2,\dots,7
let
c_{i}=\{(i-1)r+1,(i-1)r+2,\dots ir\}
.
Note that
(F^{r}X)^{\dagger_{7}}\cong(X^{\dagger 7r})^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4},c_{5},c_{6},c_{7}}
.

For any
I=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{7}\}
such that
i_{j}\in c_{j}
, and
w\in X^{\dagger 7r}
whose color is disjoint from
I
,
(X_{w}^{\dagger_{7r}})^{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{7}}\cong X_{p_{1}(w)}^{\dagger_{7}}
We note that the dimension of
X_{w}
is at least
5
from the assumption on the dimension of
X
. Thus by
Claim
2.8.4

h^{1}((X_{w}^{\dagger_{7r}})^{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{7}})=h^{1}(X_{p_{1}(w)}^{\dagger_{7}})=\Omega(h^{1}(X_{p_{1}(w)}))=\Omega(\beta).

By
Proposition
7.1.1

h^{1}((F^{r}X)^{\dagger_{7}})=\beta_{1}^{r}
for some
\beta_{1}=\Omega(\beta)
and the theorem is proven.
∎

7.4
Short detour: swap coboundary expansion of the complete complex

Claim 7.4.1
.

Let
\Delta
be the complete complex on
n
vertices, then for any
r\leqslant\frac{n}{6}
,
h^{1}(F^{r}\Delta)\geqslant\frac{1}{5}
.

The proof of this claim just follows from the fact that the faces of the complete complex has small diameter, and that every small enough cycle in it lies in the link of some other vertex. Thus one can contract any small cycle using the link.

Proof of
Claim
7.4.1
.

The group
Sym(n)
acts transitively on
F^{r}\Delta(2)
(which implies that
Aut(F^{r}\Delta)
acts transitively on the
2
-faces), therefore by
Lemma
1.6
it is enough to construct a cone of diameter
5
. Let
v_{0}=\{1,2,\dots,r\}
. Let us begin with constructing paths. For
u
such that
v_{0}\cap u=\emptyset
we set
P_{u}=(v_{0},u)
and otherwise we set
P_{u}=(v_{0},w,u)
for some
w
that is disjoint from
v_{0}\cup u
(the fact that
n\geqslant 3r
allows us to find such a
w
).

Now let us take an edge
u_{1},u_{2}
, and consider the cycle
C_{0}=P_{u_{1}}\circ(u_{1},u_{2})\circ P_{u_{2}}^{-1}
=
(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m},v_{0})
where
m\leqslant 5
. Let
A=\bigcup_{w\in C_{0}}w\subseteq[n]
. Then
|A|\leqslant r|C_{0}|\leqslant 5r
. Recall that
6r\leqslant n
and thus there exists some
x\in F^{r}\Delta
such that
x\cap A=\emptyset
and in particular, for every edge
\{a,b\}
in
C_{0}
,
\{x,a,b\}\in F^{r}\Delta(2)
. Thus we can define
T_{u_{1}u_{2}}
to be the sequence
C_{i}=(v_{0},x,v_{1},x,v_{2}\dots,x,v_{i},v_{i+1},\dots,v_{m})
for
i=1,2,\dots,m
. The loop
C_{m}
is equivalent to the trivial loop
(v_{0})
since one can contract any
(x,v_{i},x)
back to
(x)
. Thus
|T_{u_{1}u_{2}}|\leqslant 5
and by
Lemma
1.6

h^{1}(F^{r}\Delta)\geqslant\frac{1}{5}
.
∎

8
Coboundary expansion of the spherical building’s faces complex

In this section we apply the tools we developed in previous sections to lower bound the coboundary expansion of the faces complex of the spherical building.

Theorem 8.1

(More general version of
Theorem
1.4
)
.

Let
d,n
be integers such that
n>d^{5}
. There is some
q_{0}=q_{0}(d)
such that the following holds. Let
q>q_{0}
be any prime power. Let
\mathcal{S}
be either
SL_{n+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-spherical building, or a link of a
d
-face in the
SL_{n+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
spherical building. Let
\mathcal{Z}=\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\mathcal{S}
be its faces complex. Then
\mathcal{Z}
is a coboundary expander and
h^{1}(\mathcal{Z})\geqslant\exp(-O(\sqrt{d}))
.

From this theorem we immediately derive
Theorem
1.5
.

Proof of
Theorem
1.5
from
Theorem
8.1
.

Let
X
be a complex as in
Theorem
1.5
. By
Theorem
2.15
and the fact that its links are coboundary expanders (since they are isomorphic to faces complexes of links of the spherical building), it holds that
h^{1}(X)\geqslant\exp(-O(\sqrt{d}))
(as a cosystolic expander).
∎

8.1
Notation for this section

We denote by
\mathcal{Z}=\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\mathcal{S}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathrm{F}\!\mathcal{S}
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the spherical building of
SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.
For a vertex
v\in\mathcal{S}(0)
, we denote its dimension as a linear subspace by
col(v)
. We can write the link of
v
as

\mathcal{S}_{v}=\left\{s^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\left|\;\vphantom{s^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{B^{\prime}},s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{B^{\prime\prime}}}\right.s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{B^{\prime}},s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{B^{\prime\prime}}\right\}

where

B^{\prime}=\left\{i\in\mathbb{N}\left|\;\vphantom{i\in\mathbb{N}0<i<col(v)}\right.0<i<col(v)\right\},\quad B^{\prime\prime}=\left\{i\in\mathbb{N}\left|\;\vphantom{i\in\mathbb{N}col(v)<i<n}\right.col(v)<i<n\right\}.

For a general face
w=\{v_{0}<v_{1}<\cdots<v_{r}\}\in\mathcal{S}
, we can write the link

\mathcal{S}_{w}=\left\{s^{0}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\cdots\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s^{r}\in\mathcal{S}\left|\;\vphantom{s^{0}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\cdots\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s^{r}\in\mathcal{S}\forall i\in\{0,\ldots,r\},\;s^{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{B_{i}}}\right.\forall i\in\{0,\ldots,r\},\;s^{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{B_{i}}\right\}

where
B_{i}=\left\{i\in\mathbb{N}\left|\;\vphantom{i\in\mathbb{N}col(v_{i-1})<i<col(v_{i})}\right.col(v_{i-1})<i<col(v_{i})\right\}
for
0<i\leqslant r
, and
B_{0}=\left\{i\in\mathbb{N}\left|\;\vphantom{i\in\mathbb{N}0<i<col(v_{0})}\right.0<i<col(v_{0})\right\}
.

Recall that
\mathcal{S}
is
n
-partite, and let
c\subset[n]
be a set of colors. The same link decomposition holds for
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{c}
with respect to a face
w
whose colors are disjoint from
c
:

\mathcal{S}^{c}_{w}=\left\{s^{0}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\cdots\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s^{r}\in\mathcal{S}\left|\;\vphantom{s^{0}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\cdots\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s^{r}\in\mathcal{S}\forall i\in\{0,\ldots,r\},\;s^{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{B_{i}}}\right.\forall i\in\{0,\ldots,r\},\;s^{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{B_{i}}\right\}

where
B_{i}=\left\{i\in c\left|\;\vphantom{i\in ccol(v_{i-1})<i<col(v_{i})}\right.col(v_{i-1})<i<col(v_{i})\right\}
for
0<i\leqslant r
, and
B_{0}=\left\{i\in c\left|\;\vphantom{i\in c0<i<col(v_{0})}\right.0<i<col(v_{0})\right\}
.

8.2
Proof Roadmap

The proof of
Theorem
8.1
proceeds by decomposing the complex
\mathcal{Z}=\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\mathcal{S}
into smaller and smaller pieces, for which we are able to prove coboundary expansion, and so that we are able to go back up in the decomposition and deduce coboundary expansion for the entire complex. The decomposition takes the following steps (each based on a different technique):

1.

Color restriction

\mathcal{Z}\to\{\mathcal{Z}^{J}\}_{J}:
In
Section
8.3
we lower bound the coboundary expansion of
\mathcal{Z}
by the coboundary expansion of color-restricted sub-complexes
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
, for sets
J
of
\sqrt{d+1}
mutually disjoint colors.
We show that if most complexes
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
are
\exp(-O(\sqrt{d}))
-coboundary expanders, then
\mathcal{Z}
is too. This step is similar to the proof of
Theorem
2.17
which appears in
[
DD23a
]
.

2.

Trickling down the links

\mathcal{Z}^{J}\to\{\mathcal{Z}_{s}^{J^{\prime}}\}_{s}:
In
Section
8.5
we lower bound the coboundary expansion of
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
by coboundary expansion of “deep” links
\mathcal{Z}_{s}^{J^{\prime}}
. We take
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{J}
with size
|s|=|J|-5
so that
J^{\prime}=col(s)\setminus J
consists of
5
colors from
\mathcal{C}
, and
(\mathcal{Z}_{s})^{J^{\prime}}=\mathcal{Z}^{J^{\prime}}_{s}
is a
5
-partite complex. We use
Theorem
2.15
in an inductive “trickling down” manner, to deduce coboundary expansion of
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
from coboundary expansion of
\mathcal{Z}_{s}^{J^{\prime}}
.

3.

Tensor decomposition:

In section
Section
8.6
we show (
Lemma
8.4
) that
\mathcal{Z}^{J^{\prime}}_{s}\cong K\otimes\bigotimes_{i=0}^{r}\mathrm{F}^{J_{i}}\!\mathcal{S}_{s}\cong K\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{\tilde{J}}
where
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}\supset\mathcal{Z}
stands for
\mathrm{F}\!\mathcal{S}
,
K
is a complete five-partite complex and
\tilde{J}
still has five colors like
J^{\prime}
but is typically much narrower than
J^{\prime}
, where the
width
of a color set
J^{\prime}
is
|\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}J^{\prime}|=\sum_{c\in J^{\prime}}|c|
. Then by
Claim
2.8.2
we show that the coboundary expansion of
\mathcal{Z}^{J^{\prime}}_{s}
is at least a constant times the coboundary expansion of
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{\tilde{J}}_{s}
.

4.

GK decomposition:

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}^{\tilde{J}}\to\{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}^{I}\}_{I}
. We use the GK decomposition (described in
Section
5
) to repeatedly reduce the size of
|\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\tilde{J}|
by deleting an element from one of the five colors at every step. We stop either if some
c_{j}
becomes empty, or when all five colors are singletons, namely
|c_{j}|=1
for
j=1,\ldots,5
. Each step costs us a constant multiplicative decrease, so the expansion constant loses a factor that is exponential in the width of
\tilde{J}
.

5.

Spherical building:
We lower bound the coboundary expansion of
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}^{I}
when
I=\{\{i_{1}\},\{i_{3}\},\{i_{3}\},\{i_{4}\},\{i_{5}\}\}
is a set of singletons. We observe that
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}^{I}\cong\mathcal{S}_{\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s}^{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{5}\}}
, so we move to study such
5
-partite restrictions of links of the spherical building
\mathcal{S}
. This is done in
Section
8.7
by a combination of cones arguments and recursive GK-decomposition steps.

Notation and Parameters

Fix
d,n\in\mathbb{N}
so that
d^{5}\leqslant n
. Fix
m=\sqrt{d+1}
.

We let
\mathcal{S}
denote the spherical building of
SL_{n+1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. This is an
n
-partite complex whose colors are denoted
[n]
. We denote
\mathcal{Z}=\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\mathcal{S}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathrm{F}\!\mathcal{S}\supset\mathcal{Z}
. We let
\mathcal{C}=\binom{[n]}{d+1}
be the set of possible colors of vertices of
\mathcal{Z}
.

We use
u,v
to denote vertices of
\mathcal{S}
, and
w
to denote vertices of
\mathcal{Z}
, which are faces of
\mathcal{S}
. Faces of
\mathcal{Z}
are denoted by
s
.
We denote subsets of colors of
\mathrm{F}\!\mathcal{S}
that are mutually disjoint by the letters
J,I
(so
J,I\in\mathrm{F}\!\Delta
).
Let us now prove a slightly weaker statement than
Theorem
8.1
, that is, let us show that

h^{1}(\mathcal{Z})\geqslant\exp(-O(\sqrt{d}\log^{2}d)).

(8.1)

The proof of
Theorem
8.1
is almost identical, but removing the
\log^{2}d
in the exponent requires some more technicalities that obscure the main ideas in the proof. We prove the stronger version below.

Proof of
Theorem
8.1
(weaker version).

To bound
h^{1}(\mathcal{Z})
we follow the steps of the decomposition.
Let
\mathcal{J}
be the set of well-spread
J
’s per
Definition
8.3
. By
Proposition
8.4.1
, at least half of the sets
J
are in
\mathcal{J}
. Therefore, by
Lemma
8.2
,

h^{1}(\mathcal{Z})\geqslant\Omega(1)\cdot\min_{J\in\mathcal{J}}h^{1}(\mathcal{Z}^{J})

(8.2)

Fix
J\in\mathcal{J}
. By
Claim
8.5.1

h^{1}(\mathcal{Z}^{J})\geqslant\exp(-O(m))\cdot\min_{s\in\mathcal{Z}^{J}(m-6)}h^{1}(\mathcal{Z}_{s}^{J})

(8.3)

Fix any
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{J}(m-6)
. By
Corollary
8.7

h^{1}(\mathcal{Z}_{s}^{J})\geqslant const\cdot h^{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{\tilde{J}}_{s})

(8.4)

and by
Claim
2.6.1

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{\tilde{J}}_{s}\cong\mathrm{F}^{\tilde{J}}\!\mathcal{S}_{\cup s}
.

Next, denote by
\beta=\min_{w,I}h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w}^{I})

where the minimum is taken over sets
I
consisting of five singletons such that
I\cup col(s)\leqslant J
, and
w\in\mathcal{S}_{\cup s}
such that
col(w)\subseteq\cup J
and
col(w)\cap I=\emptyset
.

By
Proposition
7.1.1
,

h^{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{s}^{\tilde{J}})\geqslant const\cdot(\beta_{1})^{R}

(8.5)

where
\beta_{1}=\Omega(\beta)
and
R=\sum_{j}|\tilde{c}_{j}|
. By item 3(c) of
Definition
8.3
, for every
\tilde{c}_{j}
, the number of indices in crowded bins is at most
O\left(\frac{d\log d}{m\log m}\right)
, so in total
R=O\left(\frac{d\log d}{m\log m}\right)
.

Finally, by
Lemma
8.8
,

\beta=\min_{w,I}h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w}^{I})\geqslant\exp(-O(\log^{2}d))

(8.6)

We now plug in each equation into the previous one, to get the desired bound,

h^{1}(\mathcal{Z})\geqslant const\cdot\exp\left(-O\left(m+\frac{d\poly\log d}{m\log m}\right)\right)=\exp(-O(\sqrt{d}\log^{2}d)).

∎

Proof of
Theorem
8.1
(full version).

Our starting point is (
8.4
) in the proof above, namely that

h^{1}(\mathcal{Z})\geqslant\dots\geqslant\exp(-O(m))\min_{J\in\mathcal{J},s\in\mathcal{Z}^{J}(m-6)}h^{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{\tilde{J}}_{s}).

Fixing
J\in\mathcal{J}
and
s
, we bound
h^{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{\tilde{J}}_{s})
. By
Claim
2.6.1

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{\tilde{J}}_{s}\cong\mathrm{F}^{\tilde{J}}\!\mathcal{S}_{\cup s}
. We wish to use
Proposition
7.2.1
. Towards this, recall the definition of
T_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s},\tilde{J})
as in
Section
7.2
.
T_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s},\tilde{J})
is the largest constant such that for every choice of
J^{\prime}=\{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime},\dots,c_{5}^{\prime}\}
such that
c_{j}^{\prime}\subseteq\tilde{c}_{j}
and
\sum_{j=1}^{5}|c_{j}^{\prime}|=q
there are indexes
i_{j}\in c_{j}^{\prime}
, such that the coboundary expansion of
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w}^{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{5}})\geqslant T_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s},\tilde{J})
for every face
w\in\mathcal{S}_{\cup s}[\tilde{J}\setminus\cup J^{\prime}]
. By
Proposition
7.2.1

h^{1}(\mathrm{F}^{\tilde{J}}\!\mathcal{S}_{\cup s})\geqslant\exp(-O(R))\cdot\prod_{q=1}^{R}T_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s},\tilde{J})

where
R=\sum_{j=1}^{5}|\tilde{c}_{j}|
. As we saw in the weaker version’s proof,
R=O(\frac{d\log d}{m\log m})
. By the definition,

T_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s},\tilde{J})\geqslant\min_{w,I}h^{1}(S_{\cup s\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w}^{I})

so by
Lemma
8.8
,
T_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s},\tilde{J})\geqslant\exp(-O(\log^{2}d))
.

However, by
Claim
8.8.1
we can obtain a tighter bound on
T_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s},\tilde{J})
.
Let

q_{0}=\max_{B,c}|c\cap B|

where
B
is a
col(\cup s)
-bin and
c\in\tilde{J}
.
By
Definition
8.3

q_{0}=O\left(\frac{\log d}{\log m}\right)
and by
Claim
8.8.1
for every
q>10q_{0}
,

T_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s},\tilde{J})=\Omega(1)
. Thus

\mathrm{F}^{\tilde{J}}\!\mathcal{S}_{\cup s}\geqslant\exp(-O(R))\cdot\exp(-O(\log^{2}d))^{10q_{0}}\cdot\exp(-O(R-10q_{0})).

Plugging in
m=\sqrt{d}
we have that
q_{0}=O(1)
so this is at least

\exp(-O(R+\log^{2}d))=\exp(-O(\sqrt{d}))
.
In conclusion, we have that
h^{1}(\mathcal{Z})=\exp(-O(\sqrt{d}))
.
∎

8.3
From color restrictions to the entire complex

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
J
be a uniformly chosen set of
m

m=\sqrt{d+1}
pairwise disjoint colors from
\mathcal{C}
(namely,
J\sim\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\Delta(m-1)
). If
\ProbOp_{{J\sim\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\Delta(m-1)}}\left[h^{1}(\mathcal{Z}^{J})\geqslant\beta\right]=p
, then
h^{1}(\mathcal{Z})=\Omega(\beta p^{2})
.

The first step in proving
Lemma
8.2
, is to increase the size of
J
from
\sqrt{d+1}
to
(d+1)^{2}
. We do this because later on in the proof we will need the fact that for any set of colors
J
and color
c
, the fraction of neighbors of
s\in\mathcal{Z}[c_{1}]
in
\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
out of all edges of
s
will be almost independent of
c
. If
|J|=\sqrt{d+1}
then some colors
c
will intersect all colors
c^{\prime}\in J
non trivially (and therefore
s
will have no neighbors in
\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
), while other
c
’s won’t intersect any of the colors in
J
. By increasing the size of the colors to
(d+1)^{2}
, we make sure that for any color
c
, the fraction of colors
c^{\prime}\in J
that intersect
c
to be a negligible fraction of the colors in
J
. We use more precise arguments of this form proving smoothness of some distributions in
Claim
8.3.3
and
Claim
8.3.4
.

This is done using
Theorem
2.17
as a black box.

Claim 8.3.1
.

Let
J\sim\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\Delta((d+1)^{2}-1)
of colors. Then with probability
\frac{p}{2}
,
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
is a
\Omega(\beta p)
-coboundary expander.

Proof of
Claim
8.3.1
.

The marginal of choosing
J\sim\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\Delta((d+1)^{2}-1)
and then choosing
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
of size
\sqrt{d+1}
is just the distribution of choosing
J^{\prime}\sim\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\Delta(m-1)
. Hence by a Markov argument,

\ProbOp_{{J\sim{\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\Delta({d}^{2}-1)}}}\left[\ProbOp_{{J^{\prime}\subseteq J,|J^{\prime}|=\sqrt{d}}}\left[h^{1}(\mathcal{Z}^{J^{\prime}})\geqslant\beta\right]>\frac{p}{2}\right]\geqslant\frac{p}{2}.

For every such set of colors
J\in\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\Delta((d+1)^{2}-1)
, we claim that
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
is a
\Omega(\beta p)
-coboundary expander. We prove this via
Theorem
2.17
. The colors of
J
are mutually disjoint so
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
is
(d+1)^{2}
-partite. It is a local spectral expander since it is a color restriction of
\mathcal{Z}
and by the above, there is an
\Omega(p)
-fraction of color restrictions
\mathcal{Z}^{J^{\prime}}
that are
\beta
-coboundary expanders. By
Theorem
2.17

\mathcal{Z}^{J}
is a coboundary expander.
∎

Fix
J
with
(d+1)^{2}
colors. Denote by

E_{j}=\left\{\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{Z}(1)\left|\;\vphantom{\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{Z}(1)\left\lvert\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)\cap\{u,v\}\right\rvert=j}\right.\left\lvert\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)\cap\{u,v\}\right\rvert=j\right\},\qquad j=0,1,2.

In words, these are all the edges in
e\in\mathcal{Z}(1)
, such that exactly
j
of the vertices
v\in e
have that
col(v)\in J
.

For a fixed set of colors
J
we denote the following two distributions over triangles.

1.

T_{JJn}
is the distribution over
uvw\in\mathcal{Z}(2)
given that
u,v\in\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
and
w\notin\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
.

2.

T_{nnJ}
is the distribution over
uvw\in\mathcal{Z}(2)
given that
u,v\notin\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
and
w\in\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
.

For the rest of the proof we fix
f:\mathcal{Z}(1)\to\Gamma
. We need to find some
g:\mathcal{Z}(0)\to\Gamma
such that
\beta\dist(f,{\delta}g)\leqslant{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)
.

First we find a set of colors
J
that captures the low weight of
{\delta}f
. More formally, we need the following claim.

Claim 8.3.2
.

There exists some set
J\sim\mathrm{F}\!\Delta((d+1)^{2}-1)
colors such that:

1.

All colors in
J
are mutually disjoint and
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
is a
\Omega(\beta p)
-coboundary expander.

2.

wt_{\mathcal{Z}^{J}}({\delta}f)\leqslant O(p^{-1})wt({\delta}f)
.

3.

wt_{T_{JJn}}({\delta}f):=\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim T_{JJn}}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq Id\right]\leqslant O(p^{-1})wt({\delta}f)
.

4.

wt_{T_{nnJ}}({\delta}f):=\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim T_{nnJ}}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq Id\right]\leqslant O(p^{-1})wt({\delta}f)
.

The proof is similar to the proof in
[
DD23a
, Claim 4.3]
. We prove it in the end of the subsection.

Fix
J
to be as in
Claim
8.3.2
. We define
g
in two steps.

1.

We begin by defining
g
for
\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
. Since
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
is an
\Omega(\beta p)
-coboundary expander, there exists some
g
such that
\Omega(\beta p)\dist_{\mathcal{Z}^{J}}(f,{\delta}g)\leqslant wt_{\mathcal{Z}^{J}}({\delta}f)\leqslant O(p^{-1})wt({\delta}f)
. We take
g
to be one such function.

2.

Define
g
for
\mathcal{Z}(0)\setminus\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
by taking
g(v)=\maj_{u\in\mathcal{Z}_{v}^{J}(0)}\{f(vu)g(u)\}
to be the most popular value (ties broken arbitrarily). We note that
\mathcal{Z}_{v}^{J}(0)\neq\emptyset
because the color of
v
can intersect at most
d+1
colors in
J
because they are mutually disjoint, and this leaves at least
(d+1)^{2}-(d+1)
remaining colors to choose from.

To analyze
\dist(f,{\delta}g)
we need the following two smoothness claims regarding the different ways to choose edges with respect to
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
.

Claim 8.3.3
.

Let
J
be a set of
(d+1)^{2}
colors fixed mutually disjoint colors. Consider the following distributions over
E_{1}
:

1.

D_{0}
- The distribution where one samples an edge
uv\in\mathcal{Z}(1)
conditioned on being in
E_{1}
.

2.

d
- The distribution where one samples an edge by first sampling a triangle
uvw\sim T_{nnJ}
such that
u,w\notin\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
and
v\in\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
, and then outputting
uv
.

3.

D_{2}
- The distribution where one samples an edge by first sampling a triangle
uvw\sim T_{JJn}
such that
v,w\in\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
and
u\notin\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
, and then outputting
uv
.

Then
(D_{0},D_{2})
and
(d,D_{0})
are
\frac{1}{2}
-smooth.

In particular, this claim together with
Claim
2.1.1
implies that for every set
A\subseteq E_{1}
it holds that

\ProbOp_{{D_{0}}}\left[A\right]\leqslant 2\ProbOp_{{D_{2}}}\left[A\right].

(8.7)

and

\ProbOp_{{d}}\left[A\right]\leqslant 2\ProbOp_{{D_{0}}}\left[A\right].

(8.8)

Claim 8.3.4
.

Let
J
be a set of
(d+1)^{2}
mutually disjoint colors. Consider the following distributions over
E_{0}
:

1.

P_{0}
- The distribution where one samples an edge
uv\in\mathcal{Z}(1)
conditioned on
u,v\notin\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
.

2.

P_{1}
- The distribution where one samples an edge
uv\in\mathcal{Z}(1)
by first sampling a triangle
uvw\sim T_{nnJ}
such that
u,v\notin\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
and
w\in\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
, and then outputting
uv
.

The
(P_{0},P_{1})
are
\frac{1}{2}
-smooth.

In particular this implies that for every set
A\subseteq E_{0}
it holds that

\ProbOp_{{P_{0}}}\left[A\right]\leqslant 2\ProbOp_{{P_{1}}}\left[A\right].

(8.9)

We prove both of these claims in
Appendix
A
.

Proof of
Lemma
8.2
.

We already know that

\dist_{E_{2}}(f,{\delta}g)\leqslant O(\frac{1}{\beta p^{2}})wt({\delta}f)

(8.10)

since this follows from the coboundary expansion of
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
and the fact that
g
was chosen to minimize this distance. We show separately that
\dist_{E_{1}}(f,{\delta}g)=O(\frac{1}{\beta p^{2}})wt({\delta}f)
, and that
\dist_{E_{0}}(f,{\delta}g)=O(\frac{1}{\beta p^{2}})wt({\delta}f)
. Here the distribution over
E_{i}
is the distribution over
\mathcal{Z}(1)
conditioned on the edge being in
E_{i}
. As
\dist(f,{\delta}g)
is a convex combination of
\dist_{E_{i}}(f,{\delta}g)
, the proposition follows.

Let us begin with
\dist_{E_{1}}(f,{\delta}g)=\ProbOp_{{uv\sim D_{0}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right]
. By
Claim
8.3.3
we can replace
D_{0}
with
D_{2}
and just show that

\ProbOp_{{uv\sim D_{2}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right]\leqslant O(\frac{1}{\beta p^{2}})wt({\delta}f).

(8.11)

Fix some
v\in\mathcal{Z}(0)\setminus\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)
, and let
\varepsilon_{v}=\ProbOp_{{u\in X_{v}^{J}(0)}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right]=\ProbOp_{{u\in X_{v}^{J}(0)}}\left[g(v)\neq f(uv)g(u)\right]
.

Recall that the underlying graph of
X_{v}^{J}
is a
\Theta(1)
-edge expander. By the edge-expansion and
Claim
2.3.1
, it holds that

\ProbOp_{{u\in X_{v}^{J}(0)}}\left[g(v)\neq f(uv)g(u)\right]=O(\ProbOp_{{uw\in X_{v}^{J}(1)}}\left[f(wv)g(w)\neq f(uv)g(u)\right]).

On the other hand, if
f(vw)={\delta}g(vw)
and
{\delta}f(vuw)=Id
then
f(wv)g(w)=f(uv)g(u)
since

\displaystyle f(wv)g(w)

\displaystyle=f(uv)g(u)\Leftrightarrow

(8.12)

\displaystyle f(wv)g(w)g(u)^{-1}f(vu)=Id\Leftrightarrow

(8.13)

\displaystyle f(wv)f(uw)f(vu)=Id\Leftrightarrow

(8.14)

\displaystyle{\delta}f(uvw)=Id

(8.15)

where the third line follows from
f(vw)={\delta}g(vw)
and last line is true by the assumption that indeed
{\delta}f(vuw)=Id
. Thus,

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{{uv\sim D_{2}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\E_{{v}}\left[\varepsilon_{v}\right]

\displaystyle=O\left(\E_{{v}}\left[\ProbOp_{{u\in X_{v}^{J}(0)}}\left[g(v)\neq f(uv)g(u)\right]\right]\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\E_{{v}}\left[\ProbOp_{{uw\sim X_{v}^{J}(1)}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq Id\right]+\ProbOp_{{uw\sim X_{v}^{J}(1)}}\left[{\delta}f(uw)\neq g(uw)\right]\right]\right)

\displaystyle=O(wt_{T_{JJn}}({\delta}f)+\dist_{E_{2}}(f,{\delta}g)),

where the last line follows from the fact that choosing
v
and then a triangle
uw
as follows amounts to the distribution
T_{JJn}
. By (
8.10
)
\dist_{E_{2}}(f,{\delta}g)=O(\frac{1}{\beta p^{2}})wt({\delta}f)
and by
Claim
8.3.2

wt_{T_{JJn}}({\delta}f)=O(p^{-1})wt({\delta}f)
thus

\dist_{E_{1}}(f,{\delta}g)=O(\ProbOp_{{uv\sim D_{2}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right])=O(\frac{1}{\beta p^{2}})wt({\delta}f).

(8.16)

We move to show that
\dist_{E_{0}}(f,{\delta}g)=\ProbOp_{{uv\sim P_{0}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right]=O(\frac{1}{\beta p^{2}})wt({\delta}f)
. By
Claim
8.3.4
it is enough to show that

\ProbOp_{{uv\sim P_{1}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right]\leqslant O(\frac{1}{\beta p^{2}})wt({\delta}f).

(8.17)

P_{1}
is a marginal of
T_{nnJ}
so

\ProbOp_{{uv\sim P_{1}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right]=\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim T_{nnJ},u,v\notin\mathcal{Z}^{J}(0)}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right].

On the other hand, if
{\delta}f(uvw)=Id
,
f(vw)={\delta}g(vw)
and
f(uw)={\delta}g(uw)
then

\displaystyle{\delta}f(uvw)=Id\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle f(uv)=f(wv)f(uw)\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle f(uv)={\delta}g(wv){\delta}g(uw)\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle f(uv)=g(u)g(v)^{-1}={\delta}g(uv).

Thus

\ProbOp_{{uv\sim P_{1}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right]\leqslant\\
\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim T_{nnJ}}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq Id\right]+2\ProbOp_{{uw\sim d}}\left[f(uw)\neq{\delta}g(uw)\right]\leqslant\\
\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim T_{nnJ}}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq Id\right]+4\ProbOp_{{uw\sim D_{0}}}\left[f(uw)\neq{\delta}g(uw)\right]\leqslant\\
wt_{T_{nnJ}}({\delta}f)+4\dist_{E_{1}}(f,{\delta}g)=O(\frac{1}{\beta p^{2}})wt({\delta}f).

Where the third line follows from
Claim
8.3.3
and the last line follows from
Claim
8.3.2
and (
8.16
).
∎

Proof of
Claim
8.3.1
.

Let
h(uvw)
be the indicator that
{\delta}f(uvw)\neq Id
. Then
\E_{{uvw\sim\mathcal{Z}(2)}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{{\delta}f\neq Id}\right]={\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)
. We define three random variables. The first is
A_{0}(J)=\ProbOp_{{uvw\sim\mathcal{Z}^{J}(2)}}\left[{\delta}f(uvw)\neq Id\right]=\E_{{uvw\sim\mathcal{Z}^{J}(2)}}\left[h(uvw)\right]
. Obviously
\E_{{J}}\left[A(J)\right]={\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)
. The other two random variables will also have the same expectation.

Let
A_{JJn}(J)={\mathrm{wt}}_{T_{JJn}}({\delta}f)
. Consider the following distribution
R
:

1.

Sample
J\sim\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\Delta(d^{2}-1)
.

2.

Sample
t\sim T_{JJn}
and
t_{2}\sim T_{nnJ}
.

3.

Output
(J,t)
.

The marginal over
t
is the distribution over triangles in
\mathcal{Z}
.

Its expectation is equal to

\displaystyle\E_{{J}}\left[A_{JJn}(J)\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{{J}}\left[\E_{{t\sim T_{JJn}}}\left[h(t)\right]\right]

(8.18)

\displaystyle=\E_{{(J,t)\sim R_{JJn}}}\left[h(t)\right]

(8.19)

\displaystyle=\E_{{t\sim\mathcal{Z}(2)}}\left[h(t)\right]

(8.20)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f).

(8.21)

Similarly for
A_{nnJ}(J)={\mathrm{wt}}_{T_{nnJ}}(J)
,
\E_{{J}}\left[A_{nnJ}(J)\right]={\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)
. Thus

\E_{{J}}\left[A_{0}(J)+A_{nnJ}(J)+A_{JJn}(J)\right]\leqslant 3{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f).

The fraction of
J\sim\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\Delta(d^{2}-1)
such that
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
is a
\Omega(\beta p)
-coboundary expander is at least
\frac{p}{2}
. Thus the expectation of
A_{0}(J)+A_{nnJ}(J)+A_{JJn}(J)
over this set so
J
’s is at most
\frac{2}{p}\cdot 3{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)
. Thus there exists some fixed
J
such that
\mathcal{Z}^{J_{0}}
is a
\Omega(\beta p)
-coboundary expander, and whose value is less than the expectation, i.e. that

A_{0}(J)+A_{nnJ}(J)+A_{JJn}(J)\leqslant\frac{6}{p}{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)
. In particular,
A_{0}(J),A_{nnJ}(J),A_{JJn}(J)\leqslant\frac{6}{p}{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)
. This
J
is the set of colors we need.
∎

8.4
Well-spread colors

We define a well-spread color to have good pseudo-random properties, that is, all indices are roughly equally spaced, and interlaced with one another so that many colors will be isolated. This will facilitate the lower bounds in the next sections

Let
J\subseteq\mathcal{C}
. Recall that
\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}J=\bigcup_{c\in J}c
.

Definition 8.3

(Well-spread subsets of colors)
.

Let
J
be a set of
m
colors in
\mathcal{C}
. We say that
J
is
well-spread
if the following properties hold.

1.
