Every
c_{1},c_{2}\in J
are disjoint.

2.

For every
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in(\cup J)\cup\{0,n\}
it holds that
\left\lvert\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}\right\rvert\geqslant\frac{n}{(m(d+1))^{3}}
.

3.

For every
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
of size
\left\lvert J^{\prime}\right\rvert=5
and
\overline{J^{\prime}}=J\setminus J^{\prime}
:

(a)

For every
\ell\in\cup\overline{J^{\prime}}\cup\{0\}
there exists some
\ell^{\prime}\in\cup\overline{J^{\prime}}\cup\{n\}
such that
1<\ell^{\prime}-\ell\leqslant\frac{100n\log(d+1)}{(d+1)m}
.

(b)

For every
c\in J^{\prime}
, the number of colors
i\in c
that are in
J^{\prime}
-crowded
\overline{J^{\prime}}
-bins is at most
\frac{100(d+1)\log(d+1)}{m\log m}
.

(c)

For every
c\in J^{\prime}
and every
\overline{J^{\prime}}
-bin
B
, it holds that
\lvert B\cap c\rvert\leqslant 20\frac{\log(d+1)}{\log m}
.

We denote by
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathrm{F}^{d}\!\Delta
the set of well-spread color sets.

Proposition 8.4.1
.

Let
d,n
be such that
n\geqslant d^{5}
. Let
6\leqslant m\leqslant(d+1)
. The probability that
m
uniformly chosen colors are
well-spread
tends to
1
whenever
n\geqslant d^{5}
and
d\to\infty
.

The proof of
Proposition
8.4.1
is just a bunch of elementary probabilistic lower bounds. We prove it in full in
Appendix
A
.

8.5
Trickling Down

Claim 8.5.1
.

Let
J\in\mathcal{J}
be a well-spread set of
m
colors,
-1\leqslant i\leqslant m-6
,

h^{1}(\mathcal{Z}^{J})\geqslant exp(-O(i))\cdot\min_{s\in\mathcal{Z}^{J}(i)}h^{1}(\mathcal{Z}_{s}^{J}).

Proof.

We wish to use
Lemma
2.16
on
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
. To do so we need to argue that
\mathcal{Z}^{J}
is a good enough local spectral expander and that every link
\mathcal{Z}^{J}_{t}
is simply connected for
|t|\leqslant i+1
. Local spectral expansion follows from
Claim
2.4.3
when the subspaces in
\mathcal{Z}
are are taken with respect to a large enough field.

Therefore we need to argue that every
\mathcal{Z}^{J}_{t}
as above is simply connected. In other words we need to show that it is a
\beta
-coboundary expander for some
\beta>0
.

Fix a link
\mathcal{Z}^{J}_{t}
. Let
J^{\prime}=J\setminus col(t)
. By
Proposition
7.1.1
it is enough to show coboundary expansion of every
\mathcal{S}_{\cup t\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w}^{I}
where
I\leqslant J^{\prime}
is a set of
5
-colors and
w\in\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\cup J^{\prime}\setminus I}
. This gives us some positive bound on coboundary expansion and implies simple connectivity. This holds by
Lemma
8.9
proven later in this section.
∎

We note that even though we only needed to prove simple connectivity of the links
\mathcal{Z}^{J}_{t}
where
J
was a set of well spread colors, the requirement that the colors are well is not necessary and one can prove simple connectivity of all links in methods similar to
[
LMM16
]
.

8.6
A tensor decomposition of a generalized link

Given
w\in\mathcal{S}
let
c^{*}=col(w)\subset[n]
and write
c^{*}=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{r}\}
. Define
r+1
bins
B_{i}=\left\{j\in[n]\left|\;\vphantom{j\in[n]j_{i}<j<j_{i+1}}\right.j_{i}<j<j_{i+1}\right\}
for
0<i<r
and also
B_{0}=\{j<j_{1}\}
and
B_{r}=\{j>j_{r}\}
.

We first show that the link of the spherical building
\mathcal{S}
itself has a product structure.

Claim 8.6.1
.

For any color
c\subseteq[n]
and any flag
w\in\mathcal{S}
,

\mathcal{S}_{w}[c]=\mathcal{S}_{w}[c^{0}]\times\cdots\times\mathcal{S}_{w}[c^{r}]
, where we let
c^{i}=c\cap B_{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,r
.

Proof.

Let
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{w}[c]
. Since
col(w^{\prime})=c
and
w^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w\in\mathcal{S}
one can clearly write
w^{\prime}=w^{\prime 0}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\cdots\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime r}
where the color of
w^{\prime i}
is
c\cap B_{i}=c^{i}
, namely
w^{\prime i}\in\mathcal{S}_{w}[c^{i}]
. It is also easy to see that the map
w^{\prime}\leftrightarrow(w^{\prime 0},\ldots,w^{\prime r})
is a bijection.
∎

Next, let
J=\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\}\in\mathrm{F}\!\Delta(\ell-1)
be a set of pairwise disjoint colors
c_{j}\subseteq[n]
, and assume they are also disjoint from
c^{*}
. (In our application, we will have
|J|=\ell=5
). We move on to the structure of a generalized link
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J}_{w}
,

Lemma 8.4
.

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J}_{w}\cong\otimes_{i=0}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}
where
J_{i}=\{c_{j}\cap B_{i}\}_{j=1}^{\ell}
.

Let us denote
c_{j}^{i}=c_{j}\cap B_{i}
. Before proving the lemma we remark that
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}{J_{i}}_{w}
is an
\ell
-partite complex with the
j
-th part being
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{w}[c_{j}^{i}]
. So when we define the tensor product of two such complexes, we naturally pair the
j
-th parts with each other, as per
Definition
2.21
.

Proof.

By definition ,
\mathrm{F}^{J}\!\mathcal{S}_{w}
is
\ell
-partite with the
j
-th part consisting of vertices
\mathrm{F}^{J}\!\mathcal{S}_{w}[c_{j}]
. Viewed as faces of
\mathcal{S}
these are exactly
\mathcal{S}_{w}[c_{j}]
which equals
\mathcal{S}_{w}[c_{j}^{0}]\times\cdots\times\mathcal{S}_{w}[c_{j}^{r}]
by the previous claim. We can therefore write each
w^{\prime}_{j}\in\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J}_{w}[c_{j}]
uniquely as
{w^{\prime}}_{j}={w^{\prime}}_{j}^{0}\cup\cdots\cup{w^{\prime}}_{j}^{r}
with
{w^{\prime}}_{j}^{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{w}[c_{j}^{i}]
. We view
{w^{\prime}}_{j}^{i}
as the projection of
{w^{\prime}}_{j}
to the
i
-th coordinate, and have a bijection between
{w^{\prime}}_{j}\leftrightarrow({w^{\prime}}_{j}^{0},\ldots,{w^{\prime}}_{j}^{r})
. This is progress because
{w^{\prime}}_{j}
is a generic vertex of
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{w}^{J}
and the RHS is a generic vertex of
\prod_{i=0}^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{w}^{J_{i}}
, so we have a bijection between the vertex set of the two complexes.

Fix now two vertices
{w_{1},w_{2}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J}_{w}(0)
, and observe that they are connected by an edge iff for all
i
, the
i
-th coordinate is connected by an edge, namely
\{w_{1}^{i},w_{2}^{i}\}\in\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}(1)
. More generally,
\{w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{\ell}\}\in\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J}_{w}
if and only if for all
i
and
\{w^{i}_{0},w^{i}_{1},\ldots,w^{i}_{r}\}\in\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}
. One can verify that as measured complexes, both complexes have the same measure on the top level faces, and the lemma is proven.
∎

8.6.1
A refined decomposition

We now show that we can in fact ignore some of the bins in the decomposition above, simplifying the complex we need to analyze. We continue with the notation of
w\in\mathcal{S}
and
J=\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}\}
where the
c_{j}
’s are disjoint subsets of
[n]
as before. We also denote
I=col(w)\subset[n]
, and recall that by assumption
I
is mutually disjoint from the sets in
J
.
Recall that
c_{j}^{i}=c_{j}\cap B_{i}
.

Definition 8.5

(Crowded Bin)
.

A bin
B_{i}

crowded
if
J_{i}=(c_{1}^{i},\ldots,c_{\ell}^{i})
has more than one coordinate for which
c_{j}^{i}\neq\phi
. It is
lonely
if there is exactly one such coordinate, and otherwise it is
empty
.

When a bin is lonely or empty the corresponding component in the tensor decomposition becomes trivial,

Claim 8.6.2
.

1.

Let
i
be an index of lonely bin. Then
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}
is isomorphic to
K_{n_{1},1,\ldots,1}
.

2.

Let
i
is an index of an empty bin then
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}\cong K_{1,1,\dots,1}
.

This allows us to derive a significantly simplified form for
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{w}^{J}
,

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
R
be the indices of all crowded bins,

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J}_{{w}}\cong K\otimes\bigotimes_{i\in R}\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{{w}}

(8.22)

where
K
is some complete
\ell
-partite simplicial complex.

Proof of
Claim
8.6.2
.

Suppose the
i
-th bin is lonely. Then
J_{i}=(c_{1}^{i},\ldots,c_{\ell}^{i})
is a tuple where all but one of the colors
c_{j}^{i}\in J_{i}
are empty. Thus all but one of the
\ell
parts in
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}
have a single vertex (distinct copies of the empty face).
Let
c_{j}^{i}\in J_{i}
be the non-empty set, and set
n_{1}=|\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}[c_{j}^{i}]|
. Indeed for every
w_{j}\in\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}[c_{j}^{i}]
there is a top-level face containing
w_{j}
in the
j
-th part, and all the copies of the empty face in the other coordinates of
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}
. This is
K_{n_{1},1,1,\dots,1}
.

If the bin is
empty
then all sets in
J_{i}
are empty. Thus
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}
has a single vertex in every part, and a single top level face. This is
K_{1,1,\dots,1}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma
8.6
.

By
Lemma
8.4
we can decompose

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J}_{w}\cong\bigotimes_{i\in R}\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}\otimes\bigotimes_{i\notin R}\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}.

It is enough to show that
\bigotimes_{i\notin R}\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{J_{i}}_{w}
is a complete partite complex. By
Claim
8.6.2
every component in this tensor is itself a complete partite complex. The lemma follows from the observation that a partite tensor product of complete partite complexes is again a complete partite complex.
∎

By this lemma and
Claim
2.8.2
we derive, recalling that
\ell=5
,

Corollary 8.7
.

There is some constant
\beta=\beta_{m}>0
such that

h^{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{w}^{J})\geqslant\beta\cdot(h^{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{\tilde{J}}_{w}))

Where
\tilde{J}=\{\tilde{c}_{1},\tilde{c}_{2},\dots,\tilde{c}_{\ell}\}
and
\tilde{c}_{j}=\left\{i\in c_{j}\left|\;\vphantom{i\in c_{j}i\text{ is not in a lonely or empty bin}}\right.i\text{ is not in a lonely or empty bin}\right\}
.
∎

This shows we can deduce a bound on the coboundary expansion of
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{w}^{J}
from a bound on the coboundary expansion of its subcomplex
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{\tilde{J}}_{w}
that only contains crowded bins.

8.7
Expansion of colored spherical buildings

Recall
Definition
8.3
of the set
\mathcal{J}
of well-spread color sets, and let
J=\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{m}\}\in\mathcal{J}
and recall that we write
J^{\prime}\leqslant J
, if
J^{\prime}=\{c^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,c^{\prime}_{m}\}
where
c^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq c_{j}
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
I=\{\{i_{1}\},\ldots,\{i_{5}\}\}
, and let
s\in\mathcal{Z}
be such that
|s|=m-5
and there is a well-spread set of colors
J\in\mathcal{J}
such that
I\cup col(s)\leqslant J
. Let
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\cup s}^{\cup J}
be such that
col(w^{\prime})\cap I=\emptyset
.
Then
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{\cup s\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime}}^{I})\geqslant exp(-O(\log^{2}(d)))
.

Proof of
Lemma
8.8
.

Let us denote by
w=\cup s\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime}
. Fix some
I
as above, and let
I^{\prime}=\{i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}\}
be any four indexes inside
I
. By
Theorem
2.17
, if we show that
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{I^{\prime}}
is a
\beta
-coboundary expander, then it holds that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{I^{\prime}})\geqslant\Omega(\beta)
. So from now on we let
I=\{i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}\}
.

The coboundary expansion
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I}_{w})
depends on
I
and
w
. We address first the easier “direct” cases, and then move to the general case which is gradually reduced to the easier cases, via decomposition steps.

We begin by considering the case where the indices of
I
are in more than one
col(w)
-bin. Assume for example that
i_{0}
is separate from
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}
(the rest of the cases follow from the same argument). In this case, by
Claim
8.7.1
we will have that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{I})\geqslant\Omega(1/diam(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}}))
. If
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}
are not in all the same bin, then the diameter is constant (since you can traverse from any subspace of dimension
i_{1}
to any subspace of dimension
i_{3}
). Otherwise, they are in the same bin.
Let
[k_{0},k_{1}]
be the
col(w)
-bin that contains
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}
. Let
\tilde{i}_{j}=i_{j}-k_{0}
.
Let us write down explicitly who are the subspaces in
\mathcal{S}_{w^{\prime}}^{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}
. Let
v_{0},v_{1}\in w
be the subspaces of dimension
k_{0}
and
k_{1}
respectively. Then
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}}
contains all spaces that contain
v_{0}
and are contained in
v_{1}
of dimensions
I
.
It is easy to see that
\mathcal{S}_{w^{\prime}}^{I}
is isomorphic to a
3
-partite colored complex whose ambient space is
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{k_{1}-k_{0}}
,
with parts corresponding to dimensions
\tilde{i}_{1},\tilde{i}_{2},\tilde{i}_{3}
.
By
Claim
8.7.2
, we have that
diam(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}})=O(\tilde{i}_{3}/(\tilde{i}_{3}-\tilde{i}_{1}))
. We have that
\tilde{i}_{3}\leqslant k_{1}-k_{0}\leqslant\frac{100n\log d}{dm}
, where this inequality is by the well-spreadedness. To state this explicitly, the fact that
s\in\mathcal{Z}(m-6)
implies that any
col(\cup s)
-bin has length
\leqslant\frac{100n\log d}{dm}
by
3a
in
Definition
8.3
. The
col(w)
-bins cannot be longer since
\cup s\subseteq w
. Moreover, by item
2
in
Definition
8.3
, the distance between every two colors in
J
is at least
\frac{n}{(md)^{3}}
, so in particular
\tilde{i}_{3}-\tilde{i}_{1}=i_{3}-i_{1}\geqslant\frac{n}{(md)^{3}}
. Thus we have that
diam(\mathcal{S}_{w^{\prime}}^{\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}})\leqslant O(\poly(d))
and in this case we have that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{w^{\prime}}^{I})=\Omega(1/\poly(d))=\exp(-O(\log d))
.

Next, consider the case where all indexes of
I
are in the same bin. Similar to before, let us denote by
[k_{0},k_{1}]
be the
col(w^{\prime})
-bin that contains
I^{\prime}
. As before let
\tilde{I}=\{\tilde{i}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{3}
where
\tilde{i}_{j}=i_{j}-k_{0}
and as before
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{I}
is isomorphic to the four-partite complex
\mathcal{S}^{\tilde{I}}
whose ambient space is
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{k_{1}-k_{0}}
, with parts corresponding to dimensions
\tilde{I}
.

The proof of this case is the main challenge, and is encapsulated in
Lemma
8.9
, which shows that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\tilde{I}})\geqslant\exp(-O(\log(\frac{\tilde{i}_{3}}{\tilde{i}_{1}-\tilde{i}_{0}})\log(\frac{\tilde{i}_{3}}{\tilde{i_{0}}})))
. The calculation is similar to the first case: by item
(3a)
in
Definition
8.3
, every bin has size
O(\frac{n\log d}{dm})
so
\tilde{i}_{3}\leqslant O(\frac{n\log d}{dm})
. On the other hand, by item
2
in
Definition
8.3
, the distance between every two colors in
J
(and therefore in
I
) is at least
\frac{n}{(md)^{3}}
. In particular this implies that
\tilde{i}_{0}=i_{0}-k_{0}\geqslant\frac{n}{(md)^{3}}
and so is
\tilde{i}_{1}-\tilde{i}_{0}=i_{1}-i_{0}\geqslant\frac{n}{(md)^{3}}
. Thus
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\tilde{I}})\geqslant\exp(-O(\log^{2}(d)))
. To conclude,
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{I})\geqslant\exp(-O(\log^{2}(d)))
.
∎

8.7.1
Direct bounds

In this section we bound
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I}_{w})
in two cases which allow a direct bound. We note that our bounds in this section are more general than what we require in
Lemma
8.8
. Henceforth we assume that
w\in\mathcal{S}
is any face, including the empty face (unless the claim states that
w
is not empty).

The first bound is when not all
i\in I
are in the same bin. Namely, there is some
v\in w
such that
i_{0}<dim(v)<i_{3}
. Recall our notation:
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{I}=(\mathcal{S}_{w})^{I}
, so in case
v\in w
and
dim(v)=i\in I
, the complex is still
4
-partite, but there is only one vertex in
\mathcal{S}_{v}[i]
, namely
v
.

Claim 8.7.1

(Similar to
[
DD23a
]
)
.

Let
I=\{i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}\}
, let
w\in\mathcal{S}
be such that
col(w)\cap I=\emptyset
and let
v\in w
be
i_{0}\leqslant dim(v)\leqslant i_{3}
.

1.

If
i_{0}\leqslant dim(v)\leqslant i_{1}
then
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I}_{w})\geqslant\Omega(1/diam(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}}))
and if
i_{2}\leqslant dim(v)\leqslant i_{3}
then
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I}_{v})\geqslant\Omega(1/diam(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2}\}}))
.

2.

If
i_{1}<dim(v)<i_{2}
then
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I}_{v})=\Omega(1/d)
where
d=\min\{diam(\mathcal{S}^{\{i_{0},i_{1}\}}_{v}),diam(\mathcal{S}^{\{i_{2},i_{3}\}}_{v})\}
.

This claim is proven in
Appendix
A
using the cone machinery developed in
Section
4
. It relies on the following bound for the diameter,

Claim 8.7.2
.

Let
I\subseteq[n],|I|\geqslant 2
. Then
diam(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{I})=O(\frac{\max I}{\max I-\min I})
.

Proof of
Claim
8.7.2
.

We note that
diam(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{I})\leqslant 2+\min_{i_{1}<i_{2}\in I}diam(\mathcal{S}^{\{i_{1},i_{2}\}})
since for any pair
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{w}^{I}(0)
we can construct a path between
v,v^{\prime}
by first moving from
v
to some
u\in S[i_{1}]
, from
v^{\prime}
to
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{w}[i_{2}]
(by using two steps), and constructing a path
P
in
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\{i_{1},i_{2}\}}
from
u
to
u^{\prime}
. The resulting path
v\circ P\circ v^{\prime}
has length at most
2+diam(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{i_{1},i_{2}})
. Hence we prove that
diam(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\{i_{1},i_{2}\}})=O(\frac{i_{2}}{i_{2}-i_{1}})
for any
i_{1}<i_{2}
.

Fix some
i_{1}<i_{2}
. Let
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\{i_{1},i_{2}\}}(0)
and without loss of generality assume that
v,v^{\prime}\in S[i_{2}]
(we can do this by taking two steps as above. This again adds another constant that in taken into account in the
O
notation). Fix
A=\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots a_{\ell}\}
to be a basis for
v\cap v^{\prime}
(or
A=\emptyset
if they intersect trivially). Extend it to a basis for
v

B=A\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}B^{\prime}
where
B^{\prime}=\{b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{\ell}\}
, and to a basis for
v^{\prime}
,
C=A\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}C^{\prime}
where
C^{\prime}=\{c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{\ell}\}
. By taking two steps in
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\{i_{1},i_{2}\}}
we can go from
v
to a subspace
v^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathrm{dim}(v\cap v^{\prime\prime})\geqslant i_{1}
. In other words, we can take
i_{2}-i_{1}
vectors from
B^{\prime}
and replace them with vectors in
C^{\prime}
(e.g. if
i_{2}-i_{1}=m
we can go from
v
to
v^{\prime\prime}=span(A\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}D)
where
D=\{c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{m},b_{m+1},b_{m+1},\dots,b_{\ell}\}
. After at most
\frac{i_{2}}{i_{2}-i_{1}}
steps we can traverse this way to
v^{\prime}
.
∎

The second direct case is when all
i\in I
are in the same bin, but they have good gaps between them. In this case, constant coboundary expansion was already proven in
[
DD23a
]
. We reprove it again in
Appendix
A
since the proof in the previous paper used separate arguments for abelian and non-abelian groups. Our cone machinery gives a more compact proof of this claim (and a better quantitative bound).

Claim 8.7.3
.

Let
I=\{i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}\}
such that
i_{1}\geqslant 2i_{0}
,
i_{2}\geqslant i_{1}+i_{0}
and
i_{3}\geqslant i_{2}+2i_{1}
. Then
h^{1}(S_{w}^{I})\geqslant\frac{1}{36}
.

8.7.2
Decomposing

In this section we lower bound
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{I})
for an arbitrary set
I
consisting of
4
colors. We gradually decompose this complex into smaller pieces. Each piece is of the form
\mathcal{S}^{I^{\prime}}_{\{w\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime}\}}
for some
I^{\prime}
, and some subspace
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{w}
. Some of the pieces fall into the easy cases as above, and the remainder is then further decomposed until at last we are able to bound everything. In this subsection we suppress
w
(and reuse the letter in notation elsewhere) as it does not affect any of the calculations and claims (but we stress that the following
Lemma
8.9
applies for
\mathcal{S}_{w}
as well by the exact same proof).
Our main lemma is this,

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
I=\{i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}\}
such that
i_{3}>21
and such that
i_{j}-i_{j-1}\geqslant 3
. Then

h^{1}(S^{I})\geqslant\exp\left(-O\left(\log\left(\frac{i_{3}}{i_{1}-i_{0}}\right)\cdot\log\left(\frac{i_{3}}{i_{1}}\right)\right)\right).

Before proceeding, let us consider the proposition which is a corollary of the color swap lemma,
Lemma
8.10
, proven in
Appendix
B
.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
X
be a
d
-partite complex. Let
I=\{i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}\}
, let
i_{j}\in I
and
i^{\prime}\notin I
and let
I^{\prime}=\left(I\setminus\{i_{j}\}\right)\cup\{i_{j}^{\prime}\}
. Assume that the color swap walk between every two sides of
X
is a
\frac{1}{4}
-spectral expander.
Then

h^{1}(X^{I})\geqslant\Omega\left(h^{1}(X^{I^{\prime}})\cdot\min_{v\in{X[i^{\prime}]}}h^{1}(X_{v}^{I^{\prime}})\right)
.

In our case we take
X=\mathcal{S}
, and the following proposition is immediate.

Proposition 8.7.1
.

Let
I,i^{\prime}_{j}
and
I^{\prime}
be as above. Let
\beta=\min_{v\in\mathcal{S}(0),\;dim(v)=i^{\prime}_{j}}h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{v}^{I})
.
Let
\gamma=h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I^{\prime}})
. Then
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I})\geqslant\Omega(\beta\gamma)
.

Proof.

By
Claim
2.4.3
,
\mathcal{S}
is a
\frac{1}{4}
-spectral expander for a large enough ambient field. We can conclude by
Lemma
8.10
.
∎

Now that we have the main tool for this sub section, let us put it to good use.

Claim 8.7.4
.

Let
I=\{i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}\}
be such that
i_{3}\geqslant 21i_{0}
and
i_{1}\geqslant 2i_{0}
, then
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I})=\Omega(\frac{\max(i_{1}-2i_{0},i_{0})}{i_{3}-2i_{0}})
.

Proof of
Claim
8.7.4
.

If
i_{3}\geqslant 7i_{1}
then we are done by the following sub-claim,

Claim 8.7.5
.

Let
I=\{i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}\}
be such that
i_{3}\geqslant 7i_{1}
and
i_{1}\geqslant 2i_{0}
, then
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I})=\Omega(1)
.

Proof of
Claim
8.7.5
.

If
i_{1}+i_{0}\leqslant i_{2}\leqslant 5i_{1}
then by
Claim
8.7.3
we are done. Otherwise, we “shift”
i_{2}
to
i_{2}^{\prime}=i_{1}+i_{0}
. We let
I^{\prime}=(I\setminus\{i_{2}\})\cup\{i^{\prime}_{2}\}
. By
Proposition
8.7.1
it holds that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I})\geqslant\Omega(\min_{v}h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I}_{v})\cdot h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I^{\prime}}))
(where
v
is any space of dimension
i^{\prime}_{2}
). By
Claim
8.7.3

h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I^{\prime}})=\Omega(1)
, so let us verify that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I}_{v})
is also constant. Note that
\mathrm{dim}(v)=i_{0}+i_{1}\geqslant i_{0},i_{1}
so by
Claim
8.7.1

h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I}_{v})\geqslant\Omega(1/D)
where
D
is either the diameter of
\mathcal{S}^{i_{0},i_{1}}
or the diameter of
\mathcal{S}^{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2}}
. In both cases this is a constant because
i_{1}\geqslant 2i_{0}
(hence both diameters are
\leqslant 4
).
∎

If
i_{3}<7i_{1}
, it follows that
i_{1}\geqslant 3i_{0}
since
7i_{1}>i_{3}
and
i_{3}\geqslant 21i_{0}
(by assumption), so
i_{1}-2i_{0}\geqslant i_{0}
and we need to show that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I})=\Omega(\frac{i_{1}-2i_{0}}{i_{3}-2i_{0}})
.

Let
i^{\prime}_{1}=2i_{0}
and let
I^{\prime}=(I\setminus\{i_{1}\})\cup\{i^{\prime}_{1}\}
. By
Proposition
8.7.1
we have that
h(\mathcal{S}^{I})\geqslant\Omega(h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I^{\prime}})\cdot h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{v}^{I}))
for some
v
of dimension
\mathrm{dim}(v)=2i_{0}
. By
Claim
8.7.5
,
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I^{\prime}})=\Omega(1)
. By
Claim
8.7.1
it holds that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{v}^{I})\geqslant\Omega(1/diam(\mathcal{S}_{v}^{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}))
. We note that
\mathcal{S}_{v}^{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\cong\mathcal{S}^{i_{1}-2i_{0},i_{2}-2i_{0},i_{3}-2i_{0}}
so by
Claim
8.7.2

\Omega(1/diam(\mathcal{S}_{v}^{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}))=\Omega(\frac{i_{1}-2i_{0}}{i_{3}-2i_{0}})
. The claim follows.
∎

We describe the main idea of the proof of
Lemma
8.9
. Our goal is to recursively decompose
\mathcal{S}^{I}
, via
Proposition
8.7.1
. The basic step moves from
I=\{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}
to
I^{\prime}=\{i^{\prime}_{0},i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}
until
i_{0}
becomes small enough to bound
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I})
directly via
Claim
8.7.4
. Each basic step must take
i^{\prime}_{0}
large enough to bound
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{v}^{I})
directly via
Claim
8.7.4
.

We define a function
T:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
, by

T(x)=\lceil\max\{2x-i_{1},\frac{21}{20}x-\frac{1}{20}i_{3},1\}\rceil

(8.23)

and show that

Proposition 8.7.2
.

For every
I=\{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}
, it holds that

h^{1}(S^{\{{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\}})\geqslant c\cdot h^{1}(S^{\{{T(i_{0}),i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\}}).

(8.24)

where
c=2^{-O(\log(\frac{i_{3}}{i_{1}}))}
.

This
T
will decrease
i_{0}
, i.e.,
i_{0}^{\prime}=T(i_{0})<i_{0}
. Let
n(I)
be the minimal number of applications of
T
needed such that
i_{0}^{\prime\prime}=T^{n(I)}(i_{0})
satisfies the requirements of
Claim
8.7.4
, namely that
2i_{0}^{\prime\prime}\leqslant i_{1}
and
21i_{0}^{\prime\prime}\leqslant i_{3}
. Then by an iterated use of (
8.24
) we get that

h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{\{{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\}})\geqslant c\cdot h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{\{{T(i_{0}),i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\}})\geqslant c^{2}\cdot h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{\{{T^{2}(i_{0}),i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\}})\geqslant...\geqslant c^{n(I)}h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{\{{i_{0}^{\prime\prime},i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\}})=\Omega(c^{n(I)+1}).

Finally, to conclude we show that
n(I)=O\left(\log\left(\frac{i_{3}}{i_{1}-i_{0}}\right)\right)
.

Let
T_{1}(x)=2x-i_{1}
,
T_{3}(x)=\frac{21}{20}x-\frac{1}{20}i_{3}
so that

T(x)=\lceil\max\{T_{1}(x),T_{3}(x),1\}\rceil
.
For now, note that
T(i_{0})\leqslant i_{0}
whenever
1\leqslant i\leqslant i_{1}-1
because
T_{1}(x)<x
and
T_{3}(x)<x
whenever
x<i_{1}
. We now show a stronger shrinking property for
T
,

Claim 8.7.6
.

Let
m=2\log\left(\frac{i_{3}}{i_{1}-i_{0}+1}\right)
, then
T^{m}(i_{0})=\underbrace{T\circ T\circ\dots\circ T}_{\text{$m$ times}}(i_{0})\leqslant 1
and in particular
n(I)\leqslant m
.

The proof is an exercise in calculus, and can be found in
Appendix
A
.

Proof of
Proposition
8.7.2
.

We intend to use
Proposition
8.7.1
. Let
I=\{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}
, let
i^{\prime}_{0}=T(i_{0})
and let
I^{\prime}=(I\cup\{i^{\prime}_{0}\})\setminus\{i_{0}\}
. By
Proposition
8.7.1
it holds that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I})\geqslant\Omega(h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{I^{\prime}})\cdot h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{v}^{I}))
for some
w
of dimension
T(i_{0})\geqslant 2i_{0}-i_{1},\frac{21}{20}i_{0}-\frac{1}{20}i_{3}
.
To conclude the proposition we need to show that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{v}^{I})\geqslant 2^{-O(\log(\frac{i_{3}-2i_{0}}{\max(i_{0},i_{1}-2i_{0})}))}
. Indeed, for
j=0,1,2,3
let
\tilde{i}_{j}=i_{j}-i^{\prime}_{0}
. Observe that
\mathcal{S}_{v}^{I}\cong\mathcal{S}^{\tilde{i}_{0},\tilde{i}_{1},\tilde{i}_{2},\tilde{i}_{3}}
. We observe that
\tilde{i}_{1}\geqslant 2\tilde{i}_{0}
since unraveling the definitions of
\tilde{i}_{0},\tilde{i}_{1}
this is the same as
i_{1}-T(i_{0})\geqslant 2i_{0}-2T(i_{0})
. Moving things around this equation is equivalent to
T(i_{0})\geqslant 2i_{0}-i_{1}
which is a true statement since
T(i_{0})
is the maximum of
2i_{0}-i_{1}
and other expressions.

In addition,
\tilde{i}_{3}\geqslant 21\tilde{i}_{0}
. This is equivalent to
i^{\prime}_{0}=T(i_{0})\geqslant\frac{21}{20}i_{3}-\frac{1}{20}i_{0}
by the same reasoning as in the previous case. By
Claim
8.7.4
it holds that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{v}^{I})\geqslant 2^{-O(\log(\frac{i_{3}-2i_{0}}{\max(i_{0},i_{1}-2i_{0})}))}\geqslant 2^{-O(\log(\frac{i_{3}}{i_{1}}))}
and the proposition follows.
∎

Proof of
Lemma
8.9
(given
Claim
8.7.6
and
Proposition
8.7.2
).

We prove that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{\{{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\}})\geqslant c^{n(I)+1}
for
c=2^{-O(\log(\frac{i_{3}}{i_{1}}))}
. We do so by induction on
n=n(I)
for all sets of indexes
I
such that
n(I)=n
at once. The base case for
n(I)=0
is by
Claim
8.7.4
. Assume the claim is true for
n
, and show for
n+1
. Let
I
be such that
n(I)=n+1
. By
Proposition
8.7.2
it holds that

h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{\{{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\}})\geqslant c\cdot h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{\{{T(i_{0}),i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\}}).

It is obvious that
n(\{{T(i_{0}),i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\})=n
hence by induction

h^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{\{{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\}})\geqslant c^{n+2}.

By
Claim
8.7.6
,
n(I)\leqslant 2\log\left(\frac{i_{3}}{i_{1}-i_{0}+1}\right)
and we are done.
∎

8.8
An improved bound for large sets of colors

We prove the following claim,

Claim 8.8.1
.

Let
J\subseteq\mathrm{F}\!\Delta(4)
. Let
w\in\mathcal{S}
be such that
col(w)\cap(\cup J)=\emptyset
. Let

q_{0}=\max_{B,c}|c\cap B|

where
B
is a
col(w)
-bin and
c\in J
.
Then for all
q>10q_{0}
,
T_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{w},J)=\Omega(1)
.

Note that when
J
is well spread, and
w=\cup s
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}^{J}(m-6)
, then
q_{0}=O(1)
.

Proof.

To bound
T_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{w},J)
we need to show that there for every
J^{\prime}=\{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime},\dots,c_{5}^{\prime}\}\in\mathcal{J}_{q}
and for all
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{w}
whose color is disjoint from
I
we can find
5
colors
I=\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5}\}
such that
i_{j}\in c_{j}^{\prime}
and such that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{w\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime}}^{I})=\Omega(1)
.

Fix
q>10q_{0}
and
J^{\prime}=\{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime},\dots,c_{5}^{\prime}\}\in\mathcal{J}_{q}
. Let us prove that there exists some set
I=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{5}\}
as above with the property that there exists a
col(s)
-bin
B
with
|B\cap I|=1
. The reason we use such a set is because in this case
\mathcal{S}_{w\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime}}^{I}
is a complex as in
Corollary
2.22
. That is, if
B\cap I=\{i_{5}\}
, then for any
w^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{w\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime}}[\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\}]
and any
v\in\mathcal{S}_{\cup s\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w}[i_{5}]
,
w^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{v\}\in\mathcal{S}_{w\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime}}^{I}
(
i_{5}
sits in a different bin so the space
v
which is compatible with
w\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime}
should also be compatible with the flag
w^{\prime\prime}
that is contained in different bins). The walk between vertices and triangles in this complex is a constant expander (by
Claim
2.4.3
and
Theorem
2.5
) so by
Corollary
2.22
this implies that
h^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{w\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime}}^{I})=\Omega(1)
.

By assumption
q>10q_{0}
so there exists a color
c_{j}^{\prime}
such that
|c_{j}^{\prime}|>2q_{0}
. Without loss of generality let us assume this is
c_{5}^{\prime}
. We begin by choosing
I^{\prime}=\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\}
arbitrarily. If there are three bins
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}
so that
B_{i}\cap I^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, then at least two bins have that
|B_{i}\cap I^{\prime}|=1
. In this case no matter how we complete
I^{\prime}
to
I=I^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{i_{5}\}
, there will still be a bin
B_{i}
such that
|B_{i}\cap I^{\prime}|=1
. There are at most two bins
B_{1},B_{2}
with
B_{i}\cap I^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. By the definition of
q_{0}

|c_{5}\cap B_{i}|\leqslant q_{0}
but
|c_{5}|>2q_{0}
so there exists
i_{5}\in c_{5}\setminus(B_{1}\cup B_{2})
which we can choose.
∎

Appendix A
Outstanding Proofs

A.1
Proofs for Claims in
Section
2

Proof of
Claim
2.2.2
.

Let
H^{\prime}=(V,E\setminus E^{\prime})
be the complement subgraph. It is easy to verify that the stationary distribution of
H^{\prime}
is also equal to that of
G
. Let
\alpha=\ProbOp\left[{H}\right]
. Then it is easy to verify that

1.

A_{G}=\alpha A_{H}+(1-\alpha)A_{H^{\prime}}
where
A_{G},A_{H},A_{H^{\prime}}
are the respective adjacency operators.

2.

If
f
is perpendicular to the largest eigenvector of
A_{G}
, then it is perpendicular to the largest eigenvector of
A_{H}
and
A_{H^{\prime}}
. This follows from the fact that
A_{G},A_{H},A_{H^{\prime}}
are all self adjoint with respect to the same inner product, and they all share the same first eigenvector which is the constant function.

Let
f
be of norm
1
such that
A_{G}f=\lambda(G)f
. Then

\lambda(G)=\langle A_{G}f,f\rangle=\alpha\langle A_{H}f,f\rangle+(1-\alpha)\langle A_{H^{\prime}}f,f\rangle.

The rightmost term is upper bounded by
\langle A_{H^{\prime}}f,f\rangle\leqslant\langle f,f\rangle=1
. By
\lambda
expansion of
A_{H}
the leftmost term is bounded by
\langle A_{H}f,f\rangle\leqslant\lambda\langle f,f\rangle=\lambda
. Thus
\lambda(G)\leqslant\alpha\lambda+(1-\alpha)=1-\alpha(1-\lambda)
.
∎

Proof of
Claim
2.3.1
.

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp_{{uv\in E}}\left[\exists i\;u\in S_{i}\;\hbox{and}\;v\notin S_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ProbOp_{{uv\in E}}\left[u\in S_{i},v\notin S_{i}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\eta\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ProbOp\left[{S_{i}}\right](1-\ProbOp\left[{S_{i}}\right])

\displaystyle\geqslant\eta(1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ProbOp\left[{S_{i}}\right]^{2}).

Where the second inequality is by edge expansion. Moving things around we have

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ProbOp\left[{S_{i}}\right]^{2}\geqslant 1-\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}.

We can bound
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ProbOp\left[{S_{i}}\right]^{2}\leqslant\max_{i}\ProbOp\left[{S_{i}}\right]\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ProbOp\left[{S_{i}}\right]\leqslant\max_{i}\ProbOp\left[{S_{i}}\right]
and the claim is proven.
∎

Proof of
Claim
2.4.1
.

Sampling an edge in this walk can be done by first sampling
s\in X(j)
and then sampling two independent vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in X_{s}(0)
. Thus the probability that
v_{1},v_{2}
are in the same part
X_{s}[i]
is at most
\frac{1}{d-j-1}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Consider the subgraph
H
of
G
that contains all edges that go from vertices of different color. It is easy to see that the stationary distribution of
H
is the same as the stationary distribution of
G
, since for every face
s\in X(j)
, the marginal distributions of
v_{1},v_{2}\in X_{s}
are the same for sampling them independently, or sampling them conditioned on
v_{1},v_{2}
having different colors.

By the aforementioned
\ProbOp\left[{e\in H}\right]\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
thus by
Claim
2.2.2
, the proof will follow if we show that
H
is a
\max(\lambda^{2},\frac{1}{d-1})
-spectral expander.

Notice that
col:X(0)\to[d]
is a graph homomorphism from
G
to the complete graph on
d
vertices. It is well known that this is a
\frac{1}{d-1}
-spectral expander, thus it is enough to show that the bipartite graph induced by
L=X[i],R=X[i^{\prime}]
is a
\lambda^{2}
-spectral expander. For any
i
and
i^{\prime}
, the bipartite adjacency operator of this graph is the following convex combination

A=\frac{1}{\binom{d-2}{j}}\sum_{J\subseteq[d]\setminus\{i,i^{\prime}\}}S_{\{i\},J}S_{\{i^{\prime}\},J}^{*}

where
S_{\{i\},J}
is the bipartite adjacency operator for the
\{i\}
vs
J
colored swap walk. These are all
\lambda^{2}
-spectral expanders so the claim follows.
∎

Proof of
Claim
2.8.1
.

We define
g:X^{*}(0)\to\Gamma
to be
g(v_{*})=Id
and
g(u)=f(v_{*}u)
. We note that
f(v_{*}u)=g(u)g(v_{*})^{-1}={\delta}g(v_{*}u)
by definition. For other edges,

f(uw)=g(w)g(u)^{-1}\Leftrightarrow{\delta}f(v_{*}uw)=e
.
Thus

\dist(f,{\delta}g)=\frac{k-1}{k+1}\ProbOp_{{uw\in X(1)}}\left[f(uw)\neq{\delta}g(uw)\right]=\frac{k-1}{k+1}\ProbOp_{{uw\in X(1)}}\left[{\delta}f(v_{*}uw)\neq 0\right].

We notice that

\ProbOp_{{uw\in X(1)}}\left[{\delta}f(v_{*}uw)\neq 0\right]\leqslant 3\ProbOp_{{uwx\in X(2)}}\left[{\delta}f(uwx)\neq 0\right].

This is because if
{\delta}f(uwx)\neq 0
then at least one of the three triangles
v_{*}uw,v_{*}ux,v_{*}wx
is also not satisfied. In particular,

\dist(f,{\delta}g)\leqslant 3\frac{k-1}{k+1}wt({\delta}f).

∎

Proof of
Claim
2.8.2
.

We prove this by induction on
\ell
. When
\ell=0
then
Y=X\otimes K_{1,1,...,1}\cong X
and the claim is clear.

Assume the claim holds for
\ell
and prove for
\ell+1
. Without loss of generality
n_{0}>1
. It is enough to show that if
h^{1}(X)\geqslant\beta
, then
h^{1}(X^{\prime})\geqslant\Omega(\beta)
where
X^{\prime}
is the following simplicial complex. Its vertices are
X^{\prime}[0]=X(0)\times[n_{0}]
and
X^{\prime}[i]=X(i)
for
i\geqslant 1
. The top level faces,
X^{\prime}(k)
, are

X^{\prime}(k)=\left\{\{(v_{0},i),v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{k}\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{(v_{0},i),v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{k}\}\{v_{0},v_{1},...,v_{k}\}\in X(k)}\right.\{v_{0},v_{1},...,v_{k}\}\in X(k)\right\}

We sample a face by first sampling
i\in[n_{0}]
, and then independently sampling
\{v_{0},v_{1},...,v_{4}\}\in X(4)
.

The reason this is enough is because

X\otimes K_{n_{0},n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}\cong(X\otimes K_{n_{0},1,1,\dots,1})\otimes K_{1,n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{k}}\cong X^{\prime}\otimes K_{1,n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{k}},

so once we know that
h^{1}(X^{\prime})\geqslant\Omega(\beta)
we can continue by induction.

Indeed we intend to use
Theorem
5.4
. Let us use the following GK-decomposition
(\mathcal{Y},C,\nu,\pi)
.

1.

\mathcal{Y}=\{Y_{i}\}
where
Y_{i}
complex induced by
(X(0)\times\{i\})\cup(X^{\prime}(0)\setminus X^{\prime}[0])
.

2.

\nu
is given by sampling a top-level face
s=\{(v_{0},i_{0}),v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in X^{\prime}(k)
, and outputting
(Y_{i_{0}},t)
where
t\subseteq s
is chosen uniformly at random.

3.

The agreement complex
C
is has
C(0)=[n_{0}]
(technically it is
\{Y_{i}\}_{i\in[n_{0}]}
but we identify the vertices with
[n_{0}]
to shorten notation). For every two distinct
i,j
and
v\in Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}
there is a labeled edge
\{i,j\}_{v}
.

4.

Note that a vertex appears in the intersection if and only if
col(v)\neq 0
. Denote by
I^{\prime}=[k]\setminus\{0\}
and by
A=X^{I^{\prime}}(0)
the set of vertices that appear on a label.

5.

The distribution
\pi
is given by choosing
(u,v,w)\in\overset{\to}{X^{I^{\prime}}}(2)
, independently choosing three distinct
i,j,k
(uniformly at random) and outputting
\{i,j,k\}_{u,v,w}
.

Towards using
Theorem
5.4
, let us observe the following properties of this decomposition.

1.

The distribution
\nu|_{Y_{i}}
is the same distribution as
X
’s triangle distribution (identifying every
(v,i)\in X^{\prime}[0]
with
v\in X[0]
). It follows that every
Y_{i}
is a coboundary expander with
h^{1}(Y_{i})\geqslant\beta
.

2.

For every
v\in A
the local graph
C^{v}
is the complete graph, which is a constant edge expander.

3.

We also note that the set

A_{Y}=\left\{(v,Y_{i})\left|\;\vphantom{(v,Y_{i})v\in A,v\in Y_{i}}\right.v\in A,v\in Y_{i}\right\}=X^{I^{\prime}}(0)\times\{Y_{i}\}.

4.

Let us verify that all the smoothness relations hold.

(a)

\nu_{2}=\mu_{2,X^{\prime}}
, and in particular
(\nu_{2},\mu_{2,X^{\prime}})
are
1
-smooth.

(b)

The same holds for the pair
(\mu_{1,X^{\prime}},\nu_{1})
.

(c)

We need to show that
(\nu_{0,y},\pi_{0,y})
are
(A_{Y},\alpha)
-smooth for a constant
\alpha
. For every
(v,Y_{i})\in A_{Y}
it holds that

\ProbOp_{{\pi_{0,y}}}\left[(v,Y_{i})\right]=\frac{1}{n_{0}}\ProbOp_{{X^{I^{\prime}}(0)}}\left[v\right]=\frac{k}{k-1}\frac{1}{n_{0}}\ProbOp_{{X^{\prime}}}\left[v\right]=\frac{k}{k-1}\ProbOp_{{\nu_{0,y}}}\left[(v,Y_{i})\right].

In particular
(\nu_{0,y},\pi_{0,y})
are
(A_{Y},1)
-smooth.

(d)

Similarly
\pi_{2}
chooses a random triangle given that it doesn’t intersect
X^{\prime}[0]
, so
(\pi_{2},\mu_{2})
are
\frac{2}{5}
-smooth.

(e)

Similarly also to item
(c)
,
(\pi_{1,y},\nu_{1,y})
are
\frac{3}{5}
-smooth.

To conclude we must show that
C
is an
\gamma=\Omega(1)
-coboundary expander. we notice that
C
is a blow-up of the complete complex (all possible (unlabeled) triangles appear with uniform distribution). The complete complex is a
\beta=1
-coboundary expander
[
LMM16
]
. We therefore need to verify that the local graphs of every
i,j
are edge expanders. If this holds then by
Lemma
6.3

C
is a
\Omega(1)
-coboundary expander.

Indeed for every
\{i,j\}
, the agreement graph is just two steps in the colored swap walk of
X^{I^{\prime}}
between vertices and edges. While in partite complexes this walk doesn’t have an optimal spectral gap, it has a constant gap when
X
is a good enough
1
-sided spectral expander by
Claim
2.4.1
.

Hence by
Lemma
6.3

C
is a
\Omega(1)
-coboundary expander, and by
Theorem
5.4
we conclude that
X^{\prime}
is a
\Omega(\beta)
-coboundary expander (Since besides
\beta
, the rest of the parameters
\alpha,\gamma,\eta
is the theorem are constants).
∎

Proof of
Claim
2.8.3
.

Let
X
be such that
h^{1}(X^{\dagger_{\ell}})=\beta
and let
\Gamma
be any group. Let
f\in C^{1}(X,\Gamma)
. We will show that
\dist(f,B^{1}(X,\Gamma)\leqslant O(\beta){\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)
.

Let
\tilde{f}\in C^{1}(X^{\dagger_{\ell}},\Gamma)
be
\tilde{f}((v,i),(u,j))=f(vu)
for every
(v,i)\in X^{\dagger_{\ell}}[j]
and
(u,j)\in X^{\dagger_{\ell}}[j]
. By coboundary expansion there exists some
\tilde{g}\in C^{0}(X^{\dagger_{\ell}})
such that

\dist(\tilde{f},{\delta}\tilde{g})\leqslant\beta^{-1}{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}\tilde{f})=\beta^{-1}{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f).

(A.1)

Let
g:X(0)\to\Gamma
be
g(u)=maj_{(u,i)\in X^{\dagger_{\ell}}(0)}\tilde{g}(u,i)
. Then

\displaystyle\dist(f,{\delta}g)

\displaystyle=\ProbOp_{{uv\in X(1)}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right]

(A.2)

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp_{{uv\in X(1),i\neq j\in{[\ell]}}}\left[f(uv)\neq{\delta}\tilde{g}((u,i),(v,j))\right]+\ProbOp_{{uv\in X(1)}}\left[{\delta}\tilde{g}((u,i),(v,j))\neq{\delta}g(uv)\right]

(A.3)

\displaystyle\leqslant\ProbOp_{{(u,i),(v,j)\in X^{\dagger_{\ell}}(1)}}\left[\tilde{f}((u,i),(v,j))\neq{\delta}\tilde{g}((u,i),(v,j))\right]+2\ProbOp_{{v\in X(0),i\in{[\ell]}}}\left[g(v)\neq\tilde{g}(v,i)\right]

(A.4)

\displaystyle=\dist(\tilde{f},{\delta}\tilde{g})+2\ProbOp_{{v\in X(0),i\in{[\ell]}}}\left[g(v,i)\neq\tilde{g}(v,i)\right].

(A.5)

The first inequality is the triangle inequality. The second inequality is because
f(uv)=\tilde{f}((u,i),(v,j))
and because if
(\tilde{g}(u,i)=g(u)
and
\tilde{g}(v,j)=g(v)
then
{\delta}\tilde{g}((u,i),(v,j))={\delta}g(uv)
.
If we show that
\ProbOp_{{v\in X(0),i\in{[\ell]}}}\left[g(v)\neq\tilde{g}(v,i)\right]\leqslant 2\dist(\tilde{f},{\delta}\tilde{g})
then the claim will follow from
(
A.1
)
.

Indeed, the probability of disagreeing with the majority is upper bounded by the probability that
g
assigns different values to two random
(v,i_{1}),(v,i_{2})
, i.e.

\ProbOp_{{v\in X(0),i\in{[\ell]}}}\left[g(v)\neq\tilde{g}(v,i)\right]\leqslant\ProbOp_{{v\in X(0),i_{1},i_{2}\in{[\ell]}}}\left[\tilde{g}(v,i_{1})\neq\tilde{g}(v,i_{2})\right].

If
\tilde{g}(v,i_{1})\neq\tilde{g}(v,i_{2})
, then for every
(u,j)
such that
vu\in X(1)
,
{\delta}\tilde{g}((v,i_{1})(u,j))\neq{\delta}\tilde{g}((v,i_{2})(u,j))
. In particular, this means that either
f((v,i_{1}),(u,j)\neq{\delta}\tilde{g}((v,i_{1})(u,j))
or
f((v,i_{2}),(u,j)\neq{\delta}\tilde{g}((v,i_{2})(u,j))
. Thus

\ProbOp_{{v\in X(0),i_{1},i_{2}\in{[\ell]}}}\left[\tilde{g}(v,i_{1})\neq\tilde{g}(v,i_{2})\right]\leqslant 2\ProbOp_{{vu\in X(1),i,j\in{[\ell]}}}\left[{\delta}\tilde{g}((v,i),(u,j))\neq\tilde{f}((v,i),(u,j))\right]\leqslant 2\dist(\tilde{f},\tilde{g}).

We are done.
∎

Proof of
Claim
2.8.4
.

Let
X
a simplicial complex. We use the following GK-decomposition
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\nu,\pi)
.

1.

The subcomplexes
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{Y_{v}\left|\;\vphantom{Y_{v}v\in X(0)}\right.v\in X(0)\right\}
where
Y_{v}
is the complex induced by
\{(v,i)\}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\left\{(u,j)\left|\;\vphantom{(u,j)u\in X_{v}(0)}\right.u\in X_{v}(0)\right\}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}
will be a blow up of
X
. Edges
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}_{(v,j)}\in\mathcal{A}(1)
if
v\in X_{u_{1}u_{2}}(0)
and triangles are all
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}_{(u_{4},j_{4}),(u_{5},j_{5}),(u_{6},j_{6})}\in\mathcal{A}(2)
such that
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4},u_{5},u_{6}\}\in X(5)
and
j_{4},j_{5},j_{6}
are distinct.

3.

\pi
is given by:

(a)

Sampling
s\in X^{\dagger_{\ell}}(5)
.

(b)

Sampling two triangles independently
t_{1},t_{2}\subseteq s
.
t_{1}=\{(u_{1},i_{1}),(u_{2},i_{2}),(u_{3},i_{3})\},t_{2}=\{(u_{4},i_{4}),(u_{5},i_{5}),(u_{6},i_{6})\}
.

(c)

Randomly ordering the vertices of
t_{1},t_{2}
and outputting
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}_{(u_{4},j_{4}),(u_{5},j_{5}),(u_{6},j_{6})}
.

4.

\nu
is given by the marginal of
\pi
, that is, given
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}_{(u_{4},j_{4}),(u_{5},j_{5}),(u_{6},j_{6})}\sim\pi
we choose a
v\in\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}
uniformly at random and output
(\{(u_{4},j_{4}),(u_{5},j_{5}),(u_{6},j_{6})\},Y_{v})
.

Let us show that we can apply
Theorem
5.4
to this decomposition.

1.

Recall the definition of
Y^{*}
for some complex
Y
as in
Definition
2.19
. The distribution
\nu|_{Y_{v}}
gives that
Y_{v}\cong((X_{v})^{*})^{\dagger_{\ell}}
since every face in
Y_{v}
is connected to some
(v,i)
.
We will show that this implies that every
Y_{v}
is a constant coboundary expander below since this requires yet another GK-decomposition.
Let us deffer the following claim for later.

Claim A.1.1
.

Let
Z=Y^{*}
for some complex
Y
. Then
h^{1}(Z^{\dagger_{\ell}})=\Omega(1)
.

Given this claim
h^{1}(Y_{v})=\Omega(1)
.

2.

Every
(v,i)
appears in an intersection of two sub-complexes Therefore
A=X^{\dagger_{\ell}}(0)
.

3.

The local graph
\mathcal{A}^{(v,i)}
is (isomorphic to)
(\tilde{X}_{v})^{*}
where
\tilde{X}
is the skeleton of
5
-skeleton of
X
. This is because given
(v,i)
,
(\{Y_{u_{2}},Y_{u_{3}}\},(v,i))\sim\pi_{1,y}
by choosing some
s\ni(v,i)
, and in this case,
Y_{u_{2}},Y_{u_{3}}
are either chosen such that
u_{2}u_{3}\in X_{v}(1)
(with probability
\frac{2}{3}
) or such that one of
u_{2},u_{3}
is equal to
v
and the other is in
X_{v}(0)
. This is a constant expander whenever
X
is a
\lambda
-local spectral expander.

4.

Let us verify that all the smoothness relations hold.

(a)

\nu_{2}=\pi_{2}=\mu_{2,X^{\dagger_{\ell}}}
, and in particular
(\nu_{2},\mu_{2,X}),(\pi_{2},\mu_{2,X})
are
1
-smooth.

(b)

As
\nu
is a marginal of
\pi
,
\nu_{0,y}=\pi_{0,y},\nu_{1,y}=\pi_{1,y}
and the smoothness relations hold as well for these two.

5.

\mathcal{A}
is a blow-up of
X
. If we show that for every
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}\in X(1)
, the label graph is a constant edge-expander, it will follow that
h^{1}(\mathcal{A})=\Omega(h^{1}(X))
. Fix
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}\in X(1)
. The vertices of the label graph for
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}
are all
V=\left\{(w,k)\left|\;\vphantom{(w,k)w\in X_{vu}(0)}\right.w\in X_{vu}(0)\right\}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}(\{u_{1},u_{2}\}\times[\ell])
. Consider a set
S\subseteq V
such that
S\neq\emptyset,V
. We need to show that
\ProbOp\left[{E(S,V\setminus S)}\right]\geqslant c\ProbOp\left[{S}\right]\ProbOp\left[{V\setminus S}\right]
for some constant
c
(here
E(S,V\setminus S)
is the probability of sampling a directed edge
(((v_{1},j_{1}),(v_{2},j_{2}))
such that
(v_{1},j_{1})\in S
and
(v_{2},j_{2})\notin S
).

We note that for every
v\neq u_{1}
, there is a constant probability
c>0
to traverse from
(v,k)
to some
(u_{1},k^{\prime})
. A similar claim holds for
u_{2}
. This is because in the label graph we traverse from
(v,k)
to any triple
(u_{3},(u_{5},j_{5}),(u_{6},j_{6}))
such that
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}_{(v,k),(u_{5},j_{5}),(j_{6},j_{6})}
and then take a second (independent) step from the
(u_{3},(u_{5},j_{5}),(u_{6},j_{6}))
back to
(v^{\prime},k^{\prime})
by re completing it it a triangle
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}_{(v^{\prime},k^{\prime}),(u_{5},j_{5}),(u_{6},j_{6})}
. If
u_{5},u_{6}\neq u_{1}
(which happens with constant probability) then the probability that the completing label
(v^{\prime},k^{\prime})
is such that
v^{\prime}=u_{1}
is a constant from the definition of the distribution.

Thus for every
j\in[\ell]
,
\ProbOp\left[{(u_{1},j)}\right]\geqslant c^{\prime}
for some constant
c^{\prime}
(and the same is true for
u_{2}
). Thus fix some subset
S
and assume without loss of generality that
(u_{1},1)\notin S
(or otherwise consider the complement):

(a)

If
\ProbOp\left[{S\setminus\left\{(u_{1},j)\left|\;\vphantom{(u_{1},j)j\in{[\ell]}}\right.j\in{[\ell]}\right\}}\right]\geqslant c^{\prime}\ProbOp\left[{S}\right]
then

\displaystyle\ProbOp\left[{E(S,V\setminus S)}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp_{{((v,k),(v^{\prime},k^{\prime}))}}\left[(v,k)\in S,(v^{\prime},k^{\prime})=(u_{1},1)\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant c^{\prime}\ProbOp_{{((v,k),(v^{\prime},k^{\prime}))}}\left[(v,k)\in S\setminus\left\{(u_{1},j)\left|\;\vphantom{(u_{1},j)j\in{[\ell]}}\right.j\in{[\ell]}\right\}\right]\cdot c

\displaystyle\geqslant cc^{\prime}\ProbOp\left[{S}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant cc^{\prime}\ProbOp\left[{S}\right]\ProbOp\left[{V\setminus S}\right].

(b)

Otherwise
(u_{2},j)\notin S
for any
j\in[\ell]
since
\ProbOp\left[{(u_{2},j)}\right]>\ProbOp\left[{S\setminus\left\{(u_{1},j)\left|\;\vphantom{(u_{1},j)j\in{[\ell]}}\right.j\in{[\ell]}\right\}}\right]
. In this case similarly

\displaystyle\ProbOp\left[{E(S,V\setminus S)}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp_{{((v,k),(v^{\prime},k^{\prime}))}}\left[(v,k)\in S,v^{\prime}=u_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant c\ProbOp\left[{[}\right]((v,k),(v^{\prime},k^{\prime}))]{S}

\displaystyle\geqslant c\ProbOp\left[{S}\right]\ProbOp\left[{V\setminus S}\right].

By
Theorem
5.4

h(X^{\dagger_{\ell}})=\Omega(h^{1}(X))
.
∎

Proof of
Claim
A.1.1
.

Fix
Y
and let
Z=Y^{*}
. Denote the vertex that participates in all faces by
v_{*}
(if there is more than one we choose one arbitrarily). We use the following GK decomposition
(\mathcal{Z}=\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell},\mathcal{A},\nu,\pi)
.

1.

Z_{i}
is the complex induced by
\{(v_{*},i)\}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\left\{(u,j)\left|\;\vphantom{(u,j)j\neq i}\right.j\neq i\right\}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}
is a blow up of the complete complex over
\ell
vertices. for every
i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]
the edges are all
\{i_{1},i_{2}\}_{(u,i_{3})}
such that
i_{3}\notin\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
and
u\neq v_{*}
. The triangles are all
\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}_{(v,i_{4}),(u,i_{5}),(w,i_{6})}
such that
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{6}
are all distinct and
\{u,v,w\}\in Y(2)
(and in particular none of them are equal to
v_{*}
).

3.

The distribution
\nu
is given by choosing a top level face in
s\in Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}
, and then taking
(Y_{i},t)
such that
(v_{*},i)\in s
and
t\subseteq s
is a triangle chosen uniformly at random. We note that the marginal of this distribution is equal to the distributions over triangles in
Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}
.

4.

The distribution
\pi
is given by:

(a)

Sampling a triangle
\{u,v,w\}\in Y
.

(b)

Independently sampling a tuple of six distinct indexes
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{6})
from
[\ell]
.

(c)

Outputting
\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}_{(u,i_{4}),(v,i_{5}),(w,i_{6})}
.

We note the marginal of this distribution over the labels is the distribution over
Y^{\dagger_{\ell}}(2)
, or equivalently, the distribution over triangles in
Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}
conditioned on not containing any
(v_{*},j)
.

1.

The distribution
\nu|_{Z_{i}}
gives that
Z_{i}\cong(X^{\dagger_{\ell-1}})^{*}
. This is because whenever a triangle is chosen in
Z_{i}
, it is because a top-level face is chosen that contains
(v_{*},i)
. Thus by
Claim
2.8.1

h^{1}(Y_{i})=\Omega(1)
.

2.

The set of vertices that appear in an intersection between two
Z_{i}
’s are

A=\left\{(u,i)\left|\;\vphantom{(u,i)i\in[\ell],u\in Y(0)}\right.i\in[\ell],u\in Y(0)\right\}.

That is, all vertices such that the left coordinate is
\neq v_{*}
.

The local graph
\mathcal{A}^{v}
is the complete graph over
[\ell-1]
vertices, which is a constant edge expander.

3.

We also denote by
A_{Z}=\left\{((u,j),Z_{i})\left|\;\vphantom{((u,j),Z_{i})(u,j)\in A,i\neq j}\right.(u,j)\in A,i\neq j\right\}
.

4.

Let us verify that the smoothness relations in
Theorem
5.4
hold.

(a)

\nu_{2}=\mu_{2,Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}}
so they are
1
-smooth. The same holds for
(\mu_{2,Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}},\nu_{1})
.

(b)

\pi_{2}
is the distribution over
Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}
conditioned on not containing any
(v_{*},j)
. This event happens with probability
1-\frac{3}{\ell}\leqslant\frac{4}{7}
. Therefore
(\pi_{2},\mu_{2,Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}})
are
\frac{4}{7}
-smooth.

(c)

Let us show that
(\nu_{0,y},\pi_{0,y})
are
(A_{Z},1)
-smooth for a constant
\alpha
. Fix
((u,j),Z_{i})\in A_{Z}
and recall that
u\neq v_{*}
.

\ProbOp_{{\nu_{0,y}}}\left[((u,j),Z_{i})\right]=\ProbOp_{{Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}(0)}}\left[(u,j)\right]\frac{\ell-1}{\ell},

where the
\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}
is because conditioned on
(u,j)
,
Z_{i}
is chosen by choosing any
j\neq i
uniformly at random.

For the other distribution, the expression is similar

\ProbOp_{{\pi_{0,y}}}\left[((u,j),Z_{i})\right]=\ProbOp_{{Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}(0)}}\left[(u,j)\left|\;\vphantom{(u,j)u\neq v_{*}}\right.u\neq v_{*}\right]\frac{\ell-1}{\ell},

The conditioning only increases the probability of
u
thus
(\nu_{0,y},\pi_{0,y})
are
1
-smooth.

(d)

Finally let us see that
(\pi_{1,y},\nu_{1,y})
are
\alpha
-smooth for a constant
\alpha
. For every edge
(\{(u,i),(v,j)\},Z_{k})
, if this edge contains
(v_{*},m)
for some
m
, its probability under
\pi_{1,y}
is
0
. Otherwise, Its probability is

\ProbOp_{{\pi_{1,y}}}\left[(\{(u,i),(v_{j})\},Z_{k})\right]=\ProbOp_{{Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}(1)}}\left[\{(u,i),(v_{j})\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{(u,i),(v_{j})\}u,v\neq v_{*}}\right.u,v\neq v_{*}\right]\frac{\ell-2}{\ell},

Where the
\frac{\ell-2}{\ell}
is the probability of choosing
Z_{k}
which in this case is just the probability of choosing
k\neq i,j
.

The probability of this event under
\nu_{1,y}
is

\ProbOp_{{\nu_{1,y}}}\left[(\{(u,i),(v_{j})\},Z_{k})\right]=\ProbOp_{{Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}(1)}}\left[\{(u,i),(v_{j})\}\right]\frac{\ell-2}{\ell},

The event we are conditioning on occurs with probability
1-\frac{2}{\ell}\geqslant\frac{5}{7}
. Therefore
(\pi_{1,y},\nu_{1,y})
are
\frac{5}{7}
-smooth.

5.

The vertices of the label graph for
i,j
are all
(v,k)
such that
k\neq i,j
. We traverse from vertex to vertex via two steps in the colored swap walk from vertices to triangles of
Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}[{[\ell]\setminus\{i,j\}}]
. The complex
Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}[{[\ell]\setminus\{i,j\}}]
is a
\lambda
-one sided and partite local spectral expander for constant
\lambda
so this two step walk is a constant expander by
Claim
2.4.1
.

By
Theorem
5.4

Z^{\dagger_{\ell}}
is a
\Omega(1)
-coboundary expander.
∎

Proof of
Lemma
2.16
.

Assume that for every
s\in X(i)
,
h^{1}(X_{s})\geqslant\beta
and prove that
h^{1}(X)\geqslant\beta\exp(-Ci)
(for a large enough but fixed constant
C>0
). We prove by induction on
i
. For
i=-1
the claim holds trivially and for
i=0
this holds by
Theorem
2.15
. So we assume the claim is true for
i
and show it for faces
i+1
.

By the induction hypothesis,

h^{1}(X)\geqslant exp(-C(i-1))\cdot\min_{t\in X(i-1)}h^{1}(X_{t})

(A.6)

By assumption, for every
t\in X(i-1)
and
v\in X_{t}^{J}(0)
,
(X_{t}^{J})_{v}=X_{\{t\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{v\}\}}
, so all links of vertices in
X_{t}
are
\beta
-coboundary expanders. It follows that
X_{t}
is also a local spectral expander, that is,
X
. It then holds by
Theorem
2.15
that

h^{1}(X_{t})\geqslant\beta\cdot\frac{1-\lambda}{24}-e\lambda\geqslant\beta\exp(-C).

Plugging this expression back in the minimum in (
A.6
) gives us
h^{1}(X)\geqslant\beta\exp(-Ci)
.
∎

A.2
Proofs for Claims in
Section
8

Proof of
Proposition
8.4.1
.

We bound the probability of every item in
Definition
8.3

not occurring
by a quantity that goes to zero.

1.

The probability that
i\in c_{1},j\in c_{2}
are the same is
\frac{1}{n}
. There are at most
(m(d+1))^{2}
such pairs, so by union bound the probability that there exists such
i\in c_{1}\cap c_{2}
is at most
\frac{(m(d+1))^{2}}{n}\leqslant\frac{1}{d+1}
.

2.

Fix
\ell\in(\cup J)\cup\{0,n\}
. The probability that another
\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell
doesn’t violate this event, i.e. is sampled such that
\left\lvert\ell^{\prime}-\ell\right\rvert\geqslant\frac{n}{(m(d+1))^{3}}
is
(1-\frac{2}{(m(d+1))^{3}})
. A priori, when we sample all other
\ell^{\prime}\in(\cup J)
, they are not independent of one another (since they must be distinct). However, even when we condition on sampling some
k\leqslant m(d+1)
other
\ell^{\prime}
’s into
(\cup J)
, the probability of sampling the next
\ell^{\prime\prime}
such that
\left\lvert\ell^{\prime\prime}-\ell\right\rvert\geqslant\frac{n}{(m(d+1))^{3}}
is always at least
(1-\frac{2}{(m(d+1))^{3}}\frac{n}{n-m(d+1)})\geqslant(1-\frac{3}{m(d+1)^{3}})
since
n\geqslant d^{5}
.

To summarize, for every
\ell
, the probability that there exists some
\ell^{\prime}\in\cup J
such that
\left\lvert\ell^{\prime}-\ell\right\rvert<\frac{n}{(m(d+1))^{3}}
is upper bounded by

1-(1-\frac{3}{(m(d+1))^{3}})^{m(d+3)}.

By Bernoulli’s inequality this is at most

\leqslant 1-(1-\frac{4}{(m(d+1))^{2}})=\frac{4}{(m(d+1))^{2}}.

Union bounding for all
m(d+1)+2
elements in
(\cup J)\cup\{0,n\}
gives us the desired bound.

3.

To bound the probabilities that
3a,3b
and
3c
are violated we will calculate a bound on the probability of a single subset
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
violating these events that will be
o(m^{-5})
. Then we union bound over all possible
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
(a union bound over
\leqslant m^{5}
events). We get that all subsets
J^{\prime}
will not violate the events. Let us indeed fix some
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
and bound the probability that one of
3a,3b
or
3c
doesn’t hold.

(a)

For
\ell\in\cup\overline{J^{\prime}}\cup\{0\}
, the probability that there is no
\ell^{\prime}\in\cup\overline{J^{\prime}}\cup\{n\}
so that
1<\ell^{\prime}-\ell\leqslant\frac{100n\log(d+1)}{(d+1)m}
is at most

(1-\frac{100\log(d+1)}{(d+1)(m-5)})^{(d+1)(m-5)}\leqslant e^{-50\log(d+1)}=\frac{1}{(d+1)^{50}}.

The explanation to this probability is similar to the proof of item
2
.

(b)

Fix some
c_{1}\in J^{\prime}
. Let us look more closely on the way we sample
J
. We can sample
J
by first sampling
(d+1)m
colors into
\cup J=\{i_{0}<i_{1}<...<i_{(d+1)m-1}\}
.
Then we sample the
(d+1)
-colors that go
c_{1}
. Afterwards, out of the rest of
\cup J\setminus c_{1}
we sample the other colors
c_{2},c_{3},c_{4},c_{5}
that go into
J^{\prime}
. Finally we partition the rest of indexes sampled in to colors that go inside
\overline{J^{\prime}}
.

We call sequence of maximal consecutive indexes in
\cup J
, that were all sampled into
c_{1}

a segment of length
r+1
. I.e. a set
\{i_{j}<i_{j+1}<...<i_{j+r}\}\subseteq c_{1}
such that
i_{r-1},i_{j+r+1}\notin c_{1}
(when
j=1
there is no
i_{j-1}
so this is vacuously true, and similarly when
r+j=(d+2)m-1
). We note that the probability that there exists segment of length
r=6+\frac{\log(d+1)}{\log m}
is at most

(d+1)\cdot\ProbOp\left[{i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{r}\in c_{1}}\right]\leqslant\frac{d+2}{m^{r}}=o(m^{-5}).

(A.7)

We explain.
(d+1)
is an upper bound on the number of possible starting points of a segment. Fixing a segment that starts at
i_{1}
(for example), for every
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant r
it holds that

\ProbOp\left[i_{\ell}\in c_{1}\left|\;\vphantom{i_{\ell}\in c_{1}i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell-1}\in c_{1}}\right.i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell-1}\in c_{1}\right]=\frac{d+1-\ell}{(d+2)m-(\ell-1)}\leqslant\frac{1}{m}.

This equality follows from the fact that the even we condition on is that
c_{1}
contains
\ell-1
other indexes. Thus there are
d-\ell
remaining indexes to sample inside
c_{1}
, out of a total of
(d+1)m-(\ell-1)
. Multiplying all these probabilities given
\frac{d+2}{m^{r}}
in (
A.7
) (note that in this calculation we didn’t even take into account the fact that some indexes were supposed to be outside of
c_{1}
, but this could only decrease the bound further).

Next we bound the probability that more than
\frac{10(d+2)}{m}
of the segments appear in a
J^{\prime}
-crowded
\overline{J^{\prime}}
-bin. If a segment
\{i_{j}<i_{j+1}<...<i_{j+r}\}
is in a
J^{\prime}
-crowded
\overline{J^{\prime}}
-bin, then either
i_{j-1}\in\bigcup_{\ell=2}^{5}c_{\ell}
or
i_{j+r+1}\in\bigcup_{\ell=2}^{5}c_{\ell}
: if both
i_{j-1}
and
i_{j+r+1}
are not in
\bigcup_{\ell=2}^{5}c_{\ell}
then this means that these indexes are both in
\overline{J^{\prime}}
, which means that the bin is lonely (a similar argument holds in the edge cases where
j+r
is the last index or
j=1
). Thus we bound the number of segments where this occurs. We will show how to bound the number of segments such that
i_{j+r+1}\in\bigcup_{\ell=2}^{5}c_{\ell}
, the other event will be bounded similarly. Let
R
be the random variable that counts the number of such “bad” segments. We will bound the probability that
R
is large using a Chernoff bound for negatively correlated random variables:

Denote by
R=\sum_{\ell}R_{\ell}
where
R_{\ell}
is the indicator that the
\ell
-th segment has that
i_{j+r+1}\in\cup(J^{\prime}\setminus\{c_{1}\})
. It is easy to see that
\E[R_{\ell}]=\frac{4(d+2)}{(d+2)(m-1)}
and there are at most
(d+2)
segments so
\E[R]\leqslant\frac{4(d+2)}{m-1}\leqslant\frac{5(d+1)}{m}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\E[R_{\ell_{1}}\cdot R_{\ell_{2}}\cdot...\cdot R_{\ell_{p}}]\leqslant

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4(d+2)}{(d+2)(m-1)}\cdot\frac{4(d+2)-1}{(d+2)(m-1)-1}\cdot...\cdot\frac{4(d+2)-p+1}{(d+2)(m-1)-p+1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{4(d+2)}{(d+2)(m-1)}\right)^{p}=\left(\frac{4}{(m-1)}\right)^{p}

This is true because conditioned on
R_{\ell_{1}},R_{\ell_{2}},\dots,R_{\ell_{q}}=1
, the probability that
R_{\ell_{q+1}}=1
only decreases from the unconditional probability, we are only conditioning previous colors to be in
\overline{J^{\prime}}
. More formally, there are
4(d+2)-q
colors we still need to choose into
c_{2},\dots,c_{5}
(since the first
q
colors were already chosen), from a total of
(d+2)(m-1)-q
colors.
Hence we can use Chernoff’s bound for negatively correlated random variables and get that

\ProbOp\left[{R>\frac{10(d+1)}{m}}\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\Omega\left(\frac{(d+1)}{m}\right)\right)=o(m^{-5}).

If indeed every segment has at most
\frac{\log(d+1)}{\log m}+6
elements, then when all these events hold, the number of
i\in c_{1}
that are in a
J^{\prime}
-crowded
\overline{J^{\prime}}
-bin is at most
\frac{10(d+1)}{m}\left(\frac{\log(d+1)}{\log m}+6\right)\leqslant\frac{100(d+1)\log(d+1)}{m\log m}
. A union bound for all five
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4},c_{5}
gives us the claim.

(c)

We will show something stronger: that the number of colors in
\cup J^{\prime}
in a single
\overline{J^{\prime}}
-bin
B
is no larger than
20\frac{\log(d+1)}{\log m}
. As before, when we choose
J
and
J^{\prime}
we first choose
(d+1)m
colors
\cup J=\{i_{0}<i_{1}<\dots<i_{dm-1}\}
. Then we decide which
5(d+1)
out of
\cup J
go into
\cup J^{\prime}
, and which go to
\cup\overline{J^{\prime}}
(we do not care which index goes to which color inside this partition). In this context, let a segment of length
r+1
be a maximal sequence of consecutive indexes in
\cup J
, that were chosen into
\cup J^{\prime}
. That is some
\{i_{j}<i_{j+1}<\dots i_{j+r}\}\subseteq\cup J^{\prime}
such that both
i_{j-1},i_{j+r+1}\notin\cup J^{\prime}
(as before, if
j=0,(d+1)m-1
the index doesn’t exist and the statement is vacuously true). We need to show that there are no segments of length
20\frac{\log(d+1)}{\log m}
with high probability. As in the previous item, the probability that there exists segment of length
r=20\frac{\log(d+1)}{\log m}
in
\cup J^{\prime}
is at most

(5d+5)\cdot\ProbOp\left[{i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{r}\in c_{1}}\right]\leqslant\frac{(d+1)}{m^{r}}=o(m^{-5}).

The item follows.

∎

Proof of
Claim
8.7.1
.

Let us show the case where
i_{1}\leqslant\mathrm{dim}(v)\leqslant i_{2}
. The other two cases are similar; the same cone we define here will work in the other cases as well. Assume without loss of generality that the diameter of
S_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}}
is the minimal diameter. The automorphism group of
S_{w}^{I}
acts transitively on
S_{w}^{I}(3)
, so by
Lemma
1.6
if we construct a cone
C
with diameter
diam(C)=O(diam(S_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}}))
, this will imply that
h^{1}(S_{w}^{I})\geqslant\Omega(1/diam(S_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}}))
.

Fix any base vertex
v_{0}\in S_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}}
. For
u\in S_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}}(0)
we set
P_{u}
to be an arbitrary shortest path between
v_{0}
and
u
. We are guaranteed that
|P_{u}|\leqslant diam(S_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}})
. For
u\in S_{w}^{i_{2},i_{3}}
we note that
v_{0}u\in S_{w}^{I}(1)
so we just set
P_{u}=(v_{0},u)
.

Now for an edge
ux\in S_{w}^{I}(1)
let us give a contraction
T_{ux}
. Let
P_{0}=P_{x}\circ(x,u)\circ P_{u}^{-1}
.

1.

If
ux\in S_{w}^{i_{2},i_{3}}(1)
then
P_{0}=(v_{0},x,u,v_{0})
where
uxv_{0}\in S_{w}^{I}(2)
. In this case we just take
P_{1}=(v_{0},w,v_{0})
and
T_{ux}=(P_{0},P_{1})
.

2.

If
u\in S_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}}(0)
and
x\in S_{w}^{i_{2},i_{3}}(0)
then
P_{0}=(v_{0},x,u)\circ P_{u}^{-1}
. Denote by
P_{u}^{-1}=(v_{m}=u,v_{m-1},\dots,v_{0})
. We define a contraction
T_{ux}=(P_{0},P_{1},\dots,P_{m},)
. Here
P_{i}=(v_{0},x)\circ(v_{m-i},v_{m-i-1},\dots,v_{0})
. Indeed we note that
P_{i}\sim_{1}P_{i+1}
where the triangle
t_{P_{i},P_{i+1}}
is
xv_{m-i}v_{m-i-1}
(it is a triangle because
v_{m_{i}}v_{m-i-1}
is an edge, and for every edge
e\in S_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}}
there is a triangle
\{x\}\cup e\in S_{w}^{I}(2)
).

3.

Finally, if
ux\in S_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}}
then
P_{0}
is a cycle contained in
S_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}}
, so let us just denote it
P_{0}=(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{m},v_{0})
. Let
y\in S_{w}^{i_{2},i_{3}}(0)
be an arbitrary vertex. For every edge
e
in this cycle, it holds that
e\cup\{y\}\in S_{w}^{I}
. Therefore we can define
T_{ux}=(P_{0},P_{1},\dots,P_{m+1})
where

P_{i}=(v_{0},y,v_{1},y,v_{2},y,\dots,y,v_{i},v_{i+1},v_{i+2},\dots,v_{m},v_{0}).

Indeed the triangle
yv_{i-1}v_{i}
is the triangle that allows us to traverse from
P_{i}
to
P_{i+1}
. Finally, we note that
P_{m+1}=(v_{0},y,v_{1},y,v_{2},y,\dots,v_{m},y,v_{0})
and by a sequence of backtracking relations taking
(y,v_{i},y)
to
(y)
it is equivalent to the trivial loop.

In all cases we see that the number of
P_{i}
’s in the contractions depends only on the length of
P_{0}
, which is always at most twice the diameter of
S_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}}
. The claim follows.
∎

Proof of
Claim
8.7.3
.

The group of automorphisms acts transitively on the
3
-faces of
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{I}
so the claim will follow from existence of a cone of diameter
\leqslant 10
and
Lemma
1.6
. Fix any
v_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{w}[i_{0}]
. For every
u\in\mathcal{S}_{w}
there exists a path
P_{u}
of length
\leqslant 3
such that every
u^{\prime}\in P_{u}\setminus\{u\}
is in
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{i_{0},i_{1}}
. This path is constructed by taking some
u_{1}\subseteq u
of color
i_{0}
, and then taking two steps
(v,u_{0},u_{1},u)
for
u_{0}\supseteq v+u_{1}
(here we use the fact that
i_{1}\geqslant 2i_{0}
to promise that there exists such a
u_{0}
. Let
\{P_{u}\}_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{w}}
be a set of such paths.

Let
\{u,u^{\prime}\}
be an edge and let
C=C_{0}=P_{u}\circ(u,u^{\prime})\circ P_{u}^{-1}
. Without loss of generality assume that
u\subseteq u^{\prime}
. Let us first do the case where
u,u^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}_{w}[i_{3}]
then one can check that the sum of spaces in the cycle,
\sum_{x\in C_{0}}x
has dimension (or color)
\leqslant i_{2}+2i_{1}
: For example, assume that
col(u)=i_{1},col(u^{\prime})=i_{2}
. Then our cycle is composed of

P_{u}=(v_{0},u_{0},u_{1},u)

P_{u^{\prime}}^{-1}=(u^{\prime},u_{1}^{\prime},u_{0}^{\prime},v_{0}).

We observe that
u_{1}\subseteq u\subseteq u^{\prime}
, that
u_{0},u_{1}\subseteq u_{0}
and that
u_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq u_{0}^{\prime}
. Thus the sum of spaces is equal to
u^{\prime}+u_{0}+u_{0}^{\prime}
which has dimension at most
i_{2}+2i_{1}
. The other cases are similar.

Thus there is a subspace
x\in\mathcal{S}_{w}[i_{3}]
that this sum, and therefore
x\supseteq v_{0},u_{1},u_{1},u,u^{\prime},u_{1}^{\prime},u_{0}^{\prime},v_{0}
. In particular, every edge in the path is contained in a triangle together with
x
. By a similar sequence of cycles as in
Claim
8.7.1
we can move from
C_{0}
to

C_{m}=(v_{0},x,u_{0},x,u_{1},x,u,x,u^{\prime},x,u_{1}^{\prime},x,u_{0}^{\prime},x,v_{0})

where
m\leqslant 7
is the number of edges. This cycle contracts to the trivial cycle by backtracking relations. Therefore the diameter of
T_{uu^{\prime}}
is
\leqslant 7
in this case.

We move to the case where (say)
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{w}[i_{3}]
and claim that we can use at most three additional triangle relations to reduce to the previous case.

Indeed, start with the path
C_{0}=P_{u}\circ(u,u^{\prime})\circ P_{u}^{-1}
as above. If
u\notin\mathcal{S}_{w}[i_{0}]
then the cycle is composed of

P_{u}=(v_{0},u_{0},u_{1},u)

P_{u^{\prime}}^{-1}=(u^{\prime},u_{1}^{\prime},u_{0}^{\prime},v_{0}).

In this case
u^{\prime}\supseteq u,u_{1},u_{1}^{\prime}
. let
y\in\mathcal{S}_{w}[i_{1}]
be such that
y\supseteq u_{1}^{\prime}+u_{1}
(these subspaces are of dimension
i_{0}
so by assumption that
i_{1}\geqslant 2i_{0}
there exists such a
y
). Using the triangle
(u^{\prime},u,u_{1}),(u^{\prime},y,u_{1})
and
(u,y,u_{1}^{\prime})
we can contract
C_{0}
to

C_{3}=(u,u_{0},u_{1},y,u_{1}^{\prime},u_{0}^{\prime},u_{0}).

We contract
C_{3}
as in the previous case, by another
6
triangles. In this case
T_{uu^{\prime}}
has diameter
9
. The case where
u\in\mathcal{S}_{w}[i_{0}]
is similar (and in fact requires one less triangle).

Thus the diameter of this cone is at most
9
.
∎

Proof of
Claim
8.7.6
.

Let
S_{1}(x)=2x-i_{1}+1,S_{3}(x)=\frac{21}{20}x-\frac{1}{20}i_{3}+1
and let
S(x)=\max\{S_{1}(x),S_{3}(x)\}
. It is obvious that for any
n
,
T^{n}(x)\leqslant S^{n}(x)
hence it is enough to show that in
m
steps it holds that
S^{m}(i_{0})\leqslant 1
. The reason we do so, is that we wish to use possibly non-integral values in the analysis. It is easy to verify that when
x\in[0,i_{0}]
it holds that
S_{i}(x)
is monotone increasing and that
S_{i}(x)\leqslant x
. Moreover, we claim that for any
n
, it holds that
S^{2n}(x)\leqslant\max\{S_{1}^{n}(x),S_{3}^{n}(x)\}
:

1.

There exists some
\bar{v}\in\{1,3\}^{2n}
such that

S^{2n}(x)=S_{v_{2n}}\circ...\circ S_{v_{2}}\circ S_{v_{1}}(x).

(A.8)

2.

If the number of
3
-s in the sequence
\bar{v}
is
\geqslant n
then
S^{2n}(x)\leqslant S_{3}^{n}(x)
since removing
S_{1}
from the expression in (
A.8
), only increases the function (since
S_{1}
of the inner expression is less or equal to the expression, and the outer function is monotone).

3.

A similar argument holds if the number of
1
-s is
\geqslant n
, showing in this case that
S^{2n}(x)\leqslant S_{1}^{n}(x)
.

Thus it is enough to show that
S_{i}^{m/2}(i_{0})\leqslant 1
for both
i=1,3
. Take
S_{1}
for instance. Solving a recursion relation shows that
S_{1}^{n}(i_{0})=(i_{0}-i_{1}-1)2^{n}+(i_{1}+1)
. Obviously
S_{1}^{n}(i_{0})\leqslant 1
if
2^{n}\geqslant\frac{i_{1}}{i_{1}-i_{0}+1}
, which obviously holds when
\frac{m}{2}=n\geqslant\log(\frac{i_{3}}{i_{1}-i_{0}})
. A similar argument holds for
S_{3}
.
∎

Proof of
Claim
7.1.1
.

Fix
e_{0}=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in X^{I}
of colors (say)
i_{1},i_{2}
. For this proof we denote by
c_{1}^{\prime}=c_{1}\setminus\{i_{1}\},c_{2}^{\prime}=c_{2}\setminus\{i_{2}\}
and for all
j\geqslant 3
,
c_{j}^{\prime}=c_{j}
. Let us describe the vertices of its label graph
G_{e_{0}}
:

1.

Faces
w\in X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{3}^{\prime}]\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{4}^{\prime}]\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\dots\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{\ell}^{\prime}]
.

2.

Faces
w\in X_{v_{2}}[c_{1}]
that contain
v_{1}
. These correspond to faces
\tilde{w}\in X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{1}^{\prime}]
.

3.

Faces
w\in X_{v_{1}}[c_{2}]
that contain
v_{2}
. These correspond to faces
\tilde{w}\in X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{2}^{\prime}]
.

Let us denote the vertices by
V
, and partition them into
\ell
parts such that for
j\geqslant 3

V_{j}=X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{j}^{\prime}]
and for
j=1,2

V_{j}=\left\{\{v_{j}\}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime}\in X[c_{j}]\left|\;\vphantom{\{v_{j}\}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w^{\prime}\in X[c_{j}]w^{\prime}\in X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{j}^{\prime}]}\right.w^{\prime}\in X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{j}^{\prime}]\right\}\cong X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{j}^{\prime}]
.

Let us define a graph
H
to be a complete graph on
\ell
vertices
V^{\prime}=\{1,2,\dots,\ell\}
with self loops. Its weight function is
\mu_{H}(\{t,j\})=\ProbOp_{{e\in C^{e_{0}}}}\left[e\in E(V_{t},V_{j})\right]
. Here
E(V_{t},V_{j})
is the set of edges between
V_{t}
and
V_{j}
, and for clarity we state that this formula is also for the case of self loops, i.e.
j=t
.

By definition the map
\rho:V\to V^{\prime}
given by
\rho(v)=j
for all
v\in V_{j}
is a homomorphism. Hence by
Claim
2.2.3
if we argue that the subgraphs
E(V_{t},V_{j})
and
H
are
\lambda<1
spectral expanders then it follows that the label graph is also a spectral expander.

Let us begin with
H
. Note that
H
is connected and non-bipartite since all edges between different
j,t
have positive weight. Moreover
\ProbOp_{{e\in C^{e_{0}}}}\left[e\in E(V_{t},V_{j})\right]
only depends on
j,t
and
\ell
- not on
v_{1},v_{2}
or the colors
c_{i}
. Thus
H
is a single graph that is connected and non-bipartite, and thus
\lvert\lambda\rvert(H)<1
.

Moving on to the subgraphs
E(V_{j},V_{t})
. There are five cases to consider.

1.

j=t\in\{1,2\}
.

2.

j=t\in\{3,4,\dots,\ell\}
.

3.

j\neq t
,
j\in\{1,2\}
and
t\in\{3,4,\dots,\ell\}
(or vice versa).

4.

j=1,t=2
(or vice versa).

5.

j\neq t,j,t\in\{3,4,\dots,\ell\}
.

In all these cases we can partition the graph
E(V_{j},V_{t})
to (a subset of) the following subgraphs with the intention of using
Claim
2.2.2
.

1.

H_{v_{3}\in w}=(V_{j},V_{t},E_{j,t,v_{3}\in w})
, the graph induced by edges
\{w,w^{\prime}\}
such that
w\in V_{j},w^{\prime}\in V_{t}
, that come from traversing through triangles
t_{1}=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}_{w,w_{2},w_{3}}
and
t_{2}=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}_{w^{\prime},w_{2},w_{3}}

such that
v_{3}\in w
.

2.

H_{v_{3}\in w^{\prime}}=(V_{j},V_{t},E_{j,t,v_{3}\in w^{\prime}})
, the graph induced by edges
\{w,w^{\prime}\}
such that
w\in V_{j},w^{\prime}\in V_{t}
, that come from traversing through triangles
t_{1}=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}_{w,w_{2},w_{3}}
and
t_{2}=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}_{w^{\prime},w_{2},w_{3}}

such that
v_{3}\in w^{\prime}
.

3.

H_{v_{3}\notin w,w^{\prime}}=(V_{j},V_{t},E_{j,t,w\notin w,w^{\prime}})
, the graph induced by edges such that
v_{3}\notin w,w^{\prime}
.

With respect to the cases above for
j
and
t
, the first graph appears as a subgraph whenever
j\notin\{1,2\}
, the second appears whenever
t\notin\{1,2\}
. The third graph appears in all cases.

Let us verify that we can indeed use
Claim
2.2.2
. In all cases, we observe that the stationary distribution of all graphs is the same: if
j\neq t
it is just the distribution of the bipartite graph between
X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{j}^{\prime}]
and
X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{t}^{\prime}]
in
X
, and if
j=t
it is the distribution over
X_{v_{1},v_{2}}[c_{j}^{\prime}]
.

We show that is a spectral expander and that for any
j,t
,
\ProbOp_{{e\in E(V_{j},V_{t})}}\left[e\in H_{v_{3}\notin w,w^{\prime}}\right]
is lower bounded by a constant (independent of the colors). Indeed, we note that in this case, traversing from
w
to
w^{\prime}
is done by first sampling
w_{2},w_{3},v_{3}\in X_{v_{1},v_{2}}
, and then independently sampling
w\in X_{u}[c_{j}^{\prime}],w^{\prime}\in X_{u}[c_{t}^{\prime}]
where
u=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}\cup s_{2}\cup s_{3}
. This is a convex combination of two steps in colored swap walks, and there for a spectral expander by the assumption that
X
is a
\frac{1}{2r^{2}}
local spectral expander. We note that this is a convex combination of swap walks and not just one swap walk since, the colors of
w_{2}
and
w_{3}
are sometimes not determined, and in addition in some cases
v_{3}\in w_{2}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w_{3}
and some times it is not, but all this doesn’t matter for the analysis.

Let us argue that
H_{v_{3}\notin w,w^{\prime}}
appears with constant probability for any
j,t
. For any
j,t
, there is constant probability that
w_{2}
or
w_{3}
are in
\bigcup_{m=3}^{\ell}V_{m}
and that
v_{3}\in w_{2}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}w_{3}
. This implies that
E(V_{j},V_{t})
is a constant expander by
Claim
2.2.2
.
∎

Proof of
Claim
8.3.3
.

Fix
uv\in\Omega_{1}
.
Note that we can describe all three probability distributions by first choosing the colors of the vertices
u,v
and then choosing the vertices themselves uniformly at random given those colors. Thus, if we denote by
B_{c_{1},c_{2}}
the set of edges between colors
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, we have that
\ProbOp_{{D_{i}}}\left[uv\right]=\frac{1}{\left\lvert B_{col(u),col(v)}\right\rvert}\ProbOp_{{D_{i}}}\left[B_{col(u),col(v)}\right]
. Hence it is enough to show for every two colors
c_{1}\in J,c_{2}\notin J
that

\ProbOp_{{D_{0}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]\leqslant 2\ProbOp_{{D_{2}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]\;\hbox{and}\;\ProbOp_{{d}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]\leqslant 2\ProbOp_{{D_{0}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right].

If
c_{1}\cap c_{2}\neq\emptyset
then all probabilities in question are
0
. Otherwise, we calculate and bound. First,

\ProbOp_{{D_{0}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]=d^{-2}\left(\binom{n-(d+1)}{(d+1)}-((d+1)^{2}-1)\right)^{-1}

Since
(d+1)^{-2}
is the probability of choosing
c_{1}\in J
and
\binom{n-(d+1)}{(d+1)}-((d+1)^{2}-1)
are the number of choices for
c_{2}\notin J
that is disjoint from
c_{1}
.
Let us consider
D_{2}
. Here for choosing the triangle
uvw\sim D_{2}
we choose mutually disjoint colors
c_{1},c^{\prime},c_{2}
such that
c_{1},c^{\prime}\in J
and
c_{2}\notin J
(uniformly from all such triples). Thus,

\ProbOp_{{D_{2}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]\geqslant d^{-2}(1-\frac{(d+1)}{(d+1)^{2}-1})\left(\binom{n-2(d+1)}{(d+1)}-((d+1)^{2}-2)\right)^{-1}

Since the probability of choosing
c_{1}
is
(d+1)^{-2}
, the probability of choosing
c^{\prime}\in J
such that
c^{\prime}\neq c_{1}
and
c^{\prime}\cap c_{2}=\emptyset
is at least
(1-\frac{(d+1)}{(d+1)^{2}-1})
(
c_{2}
intersects at most
(d+1)
colors in
J
since
|c_{2}|=d+1
and by mutual disjointness). Finally,
\binom{n-2(d+1)}{(d+1)}-((d+1)^{2}-2)
is the number of colors outside of
J
that are disjoint from both
c_{1},c^{\prime}
.
It is easy to verify that provided that
n\geqslant(d+1)^{3}
we have that
\frac{\ProbOp_{{D_{0}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]}{\ProbOp_{{D_{2}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]}\leqslant 2
for all
d
large enough.

Now let us consider
D_{1}
. Here for choosing the triangle
uvw\sim D_{1}
we choose mutually disjoint colors
c_{1},c^{\prime},c_{2}
such that
c_{1}\in J
and
c^{\prime},c_{2}\notin J
(uniformly from all such triples). Thus,

\ProbOp_{{d}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]\leqslant d^{-2}\left(\binom{n-2(d+1)}{(d+1)}-((d+1)^{2}-1)\right)^{-1}

since
(d+1)^{-2}
is the probability of choosing
c_{1}
, the probability of choosing some
c^{\prime}
that is disjoint from
c_{1}
is at most
1
(so we ignore it in the expression), and given
c_{1},c^{\prime}
the number of possible colors is
\binom{n-2(d+1)}{(d+1)}-((d+1)^{2}-1)
.

It is easy to see that in this case
\frac{\ProbOp_{{D_{1}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]}{\ProbOp_{{D_{0}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]}\leqslant 2
for all
d
large enough, as well.
∎

Proof of
Claim
8.3.4
.

Similar to the proof of
Claim
8.3.3
the claim will follow if we show that for every two
c_{1},c_{2}\notin J
it holds that

\ProbOp_{{P_{0}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]\leqslant 2\ProbOp_{{P_{1}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]

First we note that

\ProbOp_{{P_{0}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]\leqslant\left(\binom{n}{d+1}-(d+1)^{2}\right)^{-1}\left(\binom{n-(d+1)}{(d+1)}-(d+1)^{2}\right)^{-1}.

This is because choosing
c_{1}
happens with probability one over the number of colors not in
J
, i.e.
\binom{n}{(d+1)}-(d+1)^{2}
. Given
c_{1}
the probability of choosing
c_{2}
is at least
\binom{n-(d+1)}{(d+1)}-(d+1)^{2}
since this is the minimal number of colors disjoint from
c_{1}
that are not in
J
.
On the other hand, choosing the colors of
uvw\sim T_{nnJ}
is choosing
c^{\prime},c_{1},c_{2}
such that they are all mutually disjoint and such that
c^{\prime}\in J,c_{1},c_{2}\notin J
. Thus,

\ProbOp_{{P_{1}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]\geqslant(1-\frac{2(d+1)}{(d+1)^{2}-1})\binom{n-3(d+1)}{(d+1)}^{-2}

since choosing some
c^{\prime}\in J
disjoint from
c_{1},c_{2}
is with probability at least
(1-\frac{2(d+1)}{(d+1)^{2}-1})
, and given such a
c^{\prime}\in J
, choosing a pair
c_{1},c_{2}
that are disjoint is at least
\binom{n-3(d+1)}{(d+1)}^{-2}
.

For large enough
d
and any
n\geqslant d^{3}
, it holds that
\ProbOp_{{P_{0}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]\leqslant 2\ProbOp_{{P_{1}}}\left[B_{c_{1},c_{2}}\right]
as required.
∎

Appendix B
A Color Swapping Lemma

In this subsection we prove our color swapping lemma.

Lemma

(Restatement of
Lemma
8.10
)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-partite complex. Let
I=\{i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}\}
, let
i_{j}\in I
and
i^{\prime}\notin I
and let
I^{\prime}=\left(I\setminus\{i_{j}\}\right)\cup\{i_{j}^{\prime}\}
. Assume that the color swap walk between every two sides of
X
is a
\frac{1}{4}
-spectral expander.
Then

h^{1}(X^{I})\geqslant\Omega\left(h^{1}(X^{I^{\prime}})\cdot\min_{v\in{X[i^{\prime}]}}h^{1}(X_{v}^{I^{\prime}})\right)
.

Let us denote by
i^{\prime}=i_{j}^{\prime}
for ease of notation. This lemma follows directly from a decomposition as in
Theorem
5.4
. For a vertex
v\in X^{I^{\prime}}(0)
we define a subcomplex
Y_{v}
in our decomposition. If
col(v)\in I\cap I^{\prime}
this complex is induced by the vertices of
\{v\}\cup X_{v}^{I\setminus col(v)}
. If
col(v)=i^{\prime}
then
Y_{v}=X_{v}^{I^{\prime}}
